A Regularized Interface Method for Fluid-Poroelastic Structure Interaction Problems with Nonlinear Geometric Coupling
Jeffrey Kuan, Sunčica Čanić, Boris Muha

Abstract
We introduce a new regularized interface method for proving existence of weak solutions to nonlinear moving boundary problems with low-regularity interfaces. As a benchmark, we study a fluid-poroelastic structure interaction (FPSI) problem coupling the Navier-Stokes equations for an incompressible viscous fluid with the Biot system for a bulk poroelastic medium. The two phases occupy domains of the same spatial dimension, separated by a moving interface defined by the trace of the poroelastic displacement, which exhibits low regularity and strong geometric nonlinearities. Despite its importance in applications, no existence theory has been available for this nonlinear moving-domain setting, primarily because the lack of interface regularity precludes even the formulation of a weak solution framework. To address this gap, we (1) introduce a regularization of the Biot displacement via spatial convolution at scale δ > 0 \delta>0 , which defines regularized moving domains and interface, and (2) modify the weak formulation in a way that preserves energy consistency with the original problem. For each fixed δ > 0 \delta>0 , we prove existence of a weak solution to the resulting regularized interface problem. The proof strategy involves inserting a thin plate of thickness h > 0 h>0 at the interface, applying a time-discretization via a Lie operator splitting scheme, establishing uniform a priori bounds, and employing Aubin-Lions compactness on moving domains. The analysis is particularly involved, partly because the thin plate allows displacements in all spatial directions, which may lead to the formation of certain geometric singularities not present when only scalar (transverse) displacements are considered. Passing to the singular limit h → 0 h\to 0 with uniform-in-h estimates and Aubin–Lions-type compactness arguments yields a regularized interface weak solution. The regularization introduced in this manuscript is essential to maintain uniform geometric control of the moving interface and to accommodate vector-valued structural displacements. An accompanying work will prove weak-classical consistency , showing that in the limit δ → 0 \delta\to 0 , the regularized weak solutions recover classical solutions to the non-regularized problem when such classical solutions exist.



1
Introduction

In this manuscript, we study a benchmark fluid-poroelastic structure interaction (FPSI) problem involving the coupling between an incompressible, viscous Newtonian fluid and a bulk poroelastic structure, both occupying domains of the same spatial dimension. Our focus is on a benchmark configuration in two spatial dimensions. The fluid is modeled by the Navier-Stokes equations, while the poroelastic structure is governed by the Biot system.
We are particularly interested in a nonlinearly coupled setting in which the interface between the fluid and the poroelastic structure is determined by the trace of the displacement of the thick poroelastic medium in direct contact with the fluid.
In this setting, the fluid and the Biot problems are each posed on a priori unknown moving domains, and hence create complex geometric nonlinearities.

The central objective of this manuscript is to study the existence of finite-energy solutions for this problem. To the best of our knowledge, no such result currently exists for FPSI or FSI problems in which a thick structure occupying a domain of the same dimension as the fluid, is in direct contact with the fluid, without a separating interface possessing mass and elastic energy, and where the elastic properties of the structure are modeled by the classical second-order elasticity equations.

This lack of existence theory stems from several key mathematical challenges. First, the interface is defined by the trace of the displacement field of the poroelastic structure, which, in general, belongs only to
H^{1/2}
. This regularity is insufficient to define the interface as the graph of a continuous function, thereby obstructing even the geometric description of the fluid domain. Second, for moving-boundary FPSI problems, the weak formulation itself becomes problematic: key geometric quantities such as the Jacobian of the Lagrangian map on the Biot domain lack the regularity needed to rigorously interpret all terms in the formally derived weak formulation.

In this manuscript, we introduce a new approach, called the
regularized interface method
, which allows us to analyze a class of FPSI problems in which the fluid and poroelastic structure interact dynamically via two-way coupling along a moving interface, along which the fluid and poroelastic structure are in direct contact.
While we focus on FPSI problems, we note that the main ideas of this work can be extended to FSI problems involving purely elastic structures, and more generally, to moving boundary problems in which the regularity of the moving domains depend on potentially lower regularity solutions.

The main idea behind the
regularized interface method
is to introduce a regularization parameter
\delta
and to regularize the vector-valued Biot structure displacement
\boldsymbol{\eta}:\Omega_{b}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
via spatial convolution against a kernel with support of size
\delta
.
Because the regularized displacement
\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\delta}
is smooth, we can use
\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\delta}
in place of
\boldsymbol{\eta}
to define regularized moving fluid and Biot domains, as well as a regularized moving interface. In the weak formulation, any integrals over moving domains are then computed on these
regularized
geometric domains.

However, this is not sufficient to define a
consistent regularized interface weak formulation
, as a straightforward regularization of
\boldsymbol{\eta}
does not preserve the energy structure of the original problem. To achieve an energy-consistent formulation, certain terms in the regularized weak formulation must be modified so that its solutions satisfy the same type of energy estimate as those of the original, non-regularized problem.
After taking all this into account, the resulting formulation is referred to as the
regularized interface weak formulation
with regularization parameter
\delta
.

The goal of this manuscript is to demonstrate that this formulation is robust, in the sense that it admits a weak solution. This solution can be viewed as an
approximation
to the solution of the original, non-regularized problem.

In the upcoming manuscript, we will show that the
regularized interface
formulation introduced here is
consistent
with the original problem–not only in terms of energy, but also in the sense that, as the approximation parameter
\delta\to 0
, the approximate solution constructed in this manuscript converges to a classical solution of the original, non-regularized problem, provided such a classical solution exists.

We emphasize that the spatial regularization introduced by the regularized interface method offers an additional advantage: it enables the treatment of
vector-valued
elastic displacements. A common difficulty in fluid-structure interaction problems with vector-valued displacements is the potential for geometric degeneracy and loss of injectivity in the moving domains. For this reason, many FSI models restrict attention to scalar transverse or normal interface displacements. However, the additional regularity gained through spatial convolution of the Biot displacement yields uniform-in-time control over various spatial norms–such as Lipschitz norms–of the solution. This, in turn, ensures uniform-in-time control of the evolving geometry of the time-dependent domains, making the analysis of vector-valued displacements feasible.

To obtain existence of a weak solution to the regularized interface FPSI problem with geometric nonlinearities discussed above, we introduce a thin plate of thickness
h>0
with mass and elastic energy, positioned at the interface separating the regions of free fluid flow and Biot poroelastic medium.
We aim to show that for each fixed regularization parameter
\delta>0
, we will recover the desired weak solution of the problem in which Biot poroelastic medium is in direct contact with free fluid flow, by taking the limit, as
h\to 0
.
The introduction of a plate with thickness
h>0
is advantageous, as it enables us to build on prior results from
[
48
]
, which established the existence of weak solutions to a geometrically nonlinear FPSI problem with a plate separating the Biot and fluid domains.
More specifically, to prove the existence of weak solutions for each fixed
h>0
(and a given fixed
\delta>0
), we employ a constructive approach based on Lie operator splitting. We discretize the time interval
[0,T]
into
N
subintervals of size
\Delta t=T/N
, and then solve two subproblems on each time subinterval:
(1) a fluid/Biot subproblem, in which the fluid and Biot quantities are updated, and
(2) a plate subproblem, in which the interface dynamics are updated.

To pass to the limit as discretization parameter
\Delta t\to 0
, we use uniform bounds and compactness arguments–specifically, the Aubin–Lions compactness theorem for functions defined on moving domains. These tools allow us to extract subsequences of approximate solutions from the splitting scheme that converge as
\Delta t\to 0
. We then pass to the limit in the approximate weak formulations to obtain a regularized interface weak solution to the geometrically nonlinear FPSI problem with a reticular plate of thickness
h>0
.
We remark that this existence analysis is particularly involved, due in part to the fact that the thin plate allows for displacements in all spatial direction–a feature that is new in the analytical study of FPSI problems.

To establish existence of a regularized interface weak solution for the FPSI problem
without a plate
, we consider approximate solutions with plate thickness
h>0
and take the singular limit as
h\to 0
. We obtain uniform-in-
h
bounds and apply Aubin–Lions-type compactness arguments to extract strongly convergent subsequences. In the limit
h\to 0
, we recover a regularized interface weak solution satisfying the limiting weak formulation corresponding to the original problem with direct contact.

We remark that the
regularized interface construction is essential for this singular limit
h\to 0
to work
; without regularizing the interface, one does not obtain uniform geometric control on the dynamics of the interface separating the fluid and Biot material. This is because there are no sufficiently strong uniform bounds on the interface quantities in the limit as
h\to 0
without additional regularization, since any spatial regularization of the interface displacement arising from the presence of the plate (giving rise to plate displacements in
H^{2}(\Gamma)
) is scaled by powers of
h
due to the presence of the plate thickness in the plate elastodynamics equations, which causes us to lose uniform geometric control of the Biot-fluid interface in the limit as
h\to 0
, without interface regularization.

This manuscript is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we introduce the fluid-poroelastic structure problem under consideration and review prior work on FPSI models, contrasting our notion of
regularized interface
solutions with earlier moving-boundary approaches. Section
3
defines weak solutions on both fixed and moving domains, and Section
4
introduces the notion of a
regularized interface
weak solution. In Section
5
, we prove the main existence result for regularized interface weak solutions to the FPSI model with a moving boundary and a plate of thickness
h>0
. Section
6
then employs uniform bounds and compactness arguments to pass to the singular limit
h\to 0
, yielding a limiting
regularized interface
weak solution for the case of direct fluid-Biot contact (no plate). Finally, Section
7
summarizes the main results of this manuscript, and discusses
how the regularized interface method can be seen as a natural way for developing weak solutions to problems involving moving domains with limited geometric boundary regularity.

2
Problem description

We consider the coupled dynamical interaction between a poroelastic medium described by the Biot equations and an incompressible viscous fluid described by the incompressible Navier-Stokes equations. We pose these equations on moving (time-dependent) domains that depend on the dynamical quantities of the problem itself (namely the displacement of the Biot material). The entire Biot-fluid system will be posed on a 2D domain, where the Biot material and the fluid occupy two-dimensional domains of the same spatial dimension. The poroelastic material is modeled by the (geometrically nonlinear) Biot equations posed on a time-dependent (moving) 2D domain
\Omega_{b}(t)
occupied by the poroelastic material, and the incompressible fluid is modeled by the incompressible Navier-Stokes equations on a time-dependent (moving) 2D domain
\Omega_{f}(t)
occupied by the fluid. The dynamical quantities that we will model are:

•

The Biot quantities.
These include the Biot vector-valued displacement
\boldsymbol{\eta}
from its reference configuration (describing elastic displacements) and the scalar pore pressure
p
.

•

The fluid quantities.
These include the (divergence-free) vector-valued fluid velocity
\boldsymbol{u}
and the scalar fluid pressure
\pi
.

2.1
The geometry of the problem

The total domain occupied by both the Biot material and the fluid will be denoted by:

\hat{\Omega}:=\{\boldsymbol{x}=(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:|\boldsymbol{x}|<2\}.

See Fig.
1
.
For all times
t>0
, the time-dependent Biot and fluid domains will altogether occupy
\hat{\Omega}
, namely:

\hat{\Omega}=\Omega_{b}(t)\cup\Omega_{f}(t)\cup\Gamma(t),\qquad\text{ for all }t\geq 0,

where
\Omega_{b}(t)
is the time-dependent (two-dimensional) Biot domain,
\Omega_{f}(t)
is the time-dependent (two-dimensional) fluid domain, and
\Gamma(t)
is the (one-dimensional) moving interface along which the Biot poroelastic material and the fluid are in direct contact with each other.

Figure 1:
A diagram of the fixed domain (left) and the moving domain (right) geometry of the direct contact FPSI problem.

The moving domains will be defined by transformations of reference (fixed) domains for both the Biot material and the fluid, where the Biot reference domain
\hat{\Omega}_{b}
and the fluid reference domain
\hat{\Omega}_{f}
are defined by

\hat{\Omega}_{b}:=\{\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}=(\hat{x},\hat{y})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}:|\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}|<1\},\qquad\hat{\Omega}_{f}:=\{\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}=(\hat{x},\hat{y})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}:1<|\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}|<2\},

and the reference configuration for the fluid-structure interface is

\hat{\Gamma}:=\{\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}=(\hat{x},\hat{y})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}:|\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}|=1\}.

We will define the moving Biot domain by

\Omega_{b}(t):=\{(\hat{x},\hat{y})+\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\hat{x},\hat{y}):(\hat{x},\hat{y})\in\hat{\Omega}_{b}\},

and we will define the moving interface by

\Gamma(t)=\{(\hat{x},\hat{y})+\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}|_{\hat{\Gamma}}(\hat{x}):(\hat{x},\hat{y})\in\hat{\Gamma}\},

(1)

where the
displacement of the moving interface from its reference configuration
\hat{\Gamma}
is defined by
\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}|_{\hat{\Gamma}}
, which is to be interpreted as a trace along
\hat{\Gamma}
. We then define the moving fluid domain
\Omega_{f}(t)
to be the remaining part of the entire domain:

\Omega_{f}(t)=\hat{\Omega}\setminus(\Omega_{b}(t)\cup\Gamma(t)).

(2)

We therefore observe that the moving fluid/Biot domains and the moving interface depend on the a priori unknown Biot displacement
\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
. See Figure
1
.

Remark 2.1
.

We set a notation convention that will be used throughout the manuscript. We will use the “hat” notation for geometric domains whenever these domains are fixed reference domains (such as in
\hat{\Omega}_{b}
,
\hat{\Omega}_{f}
, and
\hat{\Omega}
), and we use the “hat” notation for physical quantities whenever they are defined on reference domains, such as the Biot displacement
\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
defined on
\hat{\Omega}_{b}
. Domains without hats or physical quantities without hats correspond to time-dependent moving domains (for example, as in the time-dependent fluid domain
\Omega_{f}(t)
).

Remark 2.2

(A remark about the geometry of
\hat{\Gamma}
)
.

In the explicit analysis of the problem, it will be useful to parametrize the reference configuration
\hat{\Gamma}
for the direct contact interface by

\hat{z}\in[0,2\pi]\to(\cos(\hat{z}),\sin(\hat{z}))\in\hat{\Gamma},

so that we regard the unit circle
\hat{\Gamma}
as a torus, or equivalently as the interval
\hat{z}\in[0,2\pi]
with periodic boundary conditions. It will be useful to regard functions on
\hat{\Gamma}
as (periodic) functions of
\hat{z}\in[0,2\pi]
. See the discussion in Section
3.2
.

2.2
The Biot subproblem

We first specify the dynamics of the Biot poroelastic material, and for this, we model the Biot displacement
\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}:\hat{\Omega}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
and the pore pressure
p:\hat{\Omega}\to{\mathbb{R}}
using the nonlinear Biot equations. The Biot displacement is related to the elastic properties of the material, and it describes the displacement of the Biot material from its reference configuration
\hat{\Omega}_{b}
. Namely, we can define an associated
Lagrangian map
to the Biot displacement
\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
, given by

\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{b}^{\eta}(\hat{x},\hat{y}):\hat{\Omega}_{b}\to\Omega_{b}(t),\qquad\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\eta}_{b}(\hat{x},\hat{y})=(\hat{x},\hat{y})+\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\hat{x},\hat{y}),\text{ for }(\hat{x},\hat{y})\in\hat{\Omega}_{b}.

(3)

We can then describe the coupled dynamics of the elastic displacement of Biot material and the flow of fluid through its pores through the nonlinear Biot equations, which is the following system of coupled equations:

\begin{cases}\rho_{b}\partial_{tt}\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}=\hat{\nabla}\cdot\hat{S}_{b}(\hat{\nabla}\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}},\hat{p})\qquad&\text{ in }\hat{\Omega}_{b},\\
\displaystyle\frac{c_{0}}{[\text{det}(\hat{\nabla}\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\eta}_{b})]\circ(\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\eta}_{b})^{-1}}\frac{D}{Dt}p+\alpha\nabla\cdot\frac{D}{Dt}\boldsymbol{\eta}=\nabla\cdot(\kappa\nabla p)\qquad&\text{ in }\Omega_{b}(t),\end{cases}

(4)

where the (visco)elastic Piola-Kirchhoff stress tensor is given by

\hat{S}_{b}(\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta},p)=2\mu_{e}\hat{\boldsymbol{D}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}})+\lambda_{e}(\hat{\nabla}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}})\boldsymbol{I}+2\mu_{v}\hat{\boldsymbol{D}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{t})+\lambda_{v}(\hat{\nabla}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{t})\boldsymbol{I}-\alpha\text{det}(\hat{\nabla}\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\eta}_{b})\hat{p}(\hat{\nabla}\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\eta}_{b})^{-t},

(5)

and where
\hat{\Phi}^{\eta}_{b}
denotes the Lagrangian map defined in (
3
). Note that the elastodynamics equation, which is the first equation in (
4
), is defined in the Lagrangian formulation on the reference domain
\hat{\Omega}_{b}
, whereas the momentum equation for the pore pressure, which is the second equation in (
4
), is defined in the Eulerian formulation on the moving domain
\Omega_{b}(t)
. In the second equation of (
4
), we can interpret the term on the right-hand side in terms of the
filtration velocity
, where the velocity
\boldsymbol{q}
of the flow through the poroelastic material is given by
Darcy’s law
via the relationship:

\boldsymbol{q}:=-\kappa\nabla p,

(6)

for a permeability constant
\kappa>0
. For the Biot material, we assume the elasticity coefficients in (
5
) satisfy
\mu_{e},\lambda_{e}>0
, and
\mu_{v},\lambda_{v}\geq 0
. We say that the Biot medium is:

\begin{cases}\textbf{poroelastic}\text{ if }\mu_{v}=0\text{ and }\lambda_{v}=0\\
\textbf{poroviscoelastic}\text{ if }\mu_{v}>0\text{ and }\lambda_{v}>0.\end{cases}

(7)

We emphasize that our analysis in terms of existence of regularized interface weak solutions holds for both cases, and we carry out the explicit analysis in the more challenging case of a purely poroelastic Biot medium.

We emphasize the difference in notation for the displacement and the pore pressure between these two equations. While
\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}:\hat{\Omega}_{b}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
and
\hat{p}:\hat{\Omega}_{b}\to{\mathbb{R}}
are defined on the reference Biot domain,
\boldsymbol{\eta}
and
p
are defined on the moving Biot domain via the Lagrangian map:

\boldsymbol{\eta}(x,y)=\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\Big((\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\eta}_{b})^{-1}(x,y)\Big),\qquad p(x,y)=\hat{p}\Big((\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\eta}_{b})^{-1}(x,y)\Big),\qquad\text{ for }(x,y)\in\Omega_{b}(t).

It will also be convenient to define the Lagrangian Biot velocity
\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
and the Eulerian Biot velocity
\boldsymbol{\xi}
:

\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}(\hat{x},\hat{y})=\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\hat{x},\hat{y})\ \ \text{ for }(\hat{x},\hat{y})\in\hat{\Omega}_{b},\qquad\boldsymbol{\xi}(x,y)=\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\Big((\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\eta}_{b})^{-1}(x,y)\Big)\ \ \text{ for }(x,y)\in\Omega_{b}(t).

(8)

We note that these definitions are in agreement with our notational convention in Remark
2.1
.

Similarly, it will at times be convenient to consider the (visco)elastic Piola-Kirchhoff stress tensor defined in (
5
) on the fixed domain
\hat{\Omega}_{b}
, on the moving domain
\Omega_{b}(t)
instead. We can do this by transforming
\hat{S}_{b}(\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta},p)
via the Piola transform (see
[
31
]
), to obtain the resulting Piola-Kirchhoff stress tensor defined in Eulerian (moving domain) coordinates:

S_{b}(\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta},p)=[\text{det}(\hat{\nabla}\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\eta}_{b})^{-1}\hat{S}_{b}(\hat{\nabla}\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}},\hat{p})(\hat{\nabla}\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\eta}_{b})^{t}]\circ(\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\eta}_{b})^{-1}\\
=\left(\frac{1}{\text{det}(\hat{\nabla}\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\eta}_{b})}\Big[2\mu_{e}\hat{D}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}})+\lambda_{e}(\hat{\nabla}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}})+2\mu_{v}\hat{\boldsymbol{D}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{t})+\lambda_{v}(\hat{\nabla}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{t})\Big](\hat{\nabla}\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\eta}_{b})^{t}\right)\circ(\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\eta}_{b})^{-1}-\alpha p\boldsymbol{I}\\
\text{ on }\Omega_{b}(t).

(9)

2.3
The fluid subproblem

We model the incompressible, viscous fluid using the incompressible Navier-Stokes equations, which describe the evolution of the fluid velocity
\boldsymbol{u}:\Omega_{f}(t)\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
and the fluid pressure
\pi:\Omega_{f}(t)\to{\mathbb{R}}
via equations for balance of momentum and conservation of mass (incompressibility) respectively:

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}+(\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{u}=\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{f}(\nabla\boldsymbol{u},\pi),\\
\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}=0,\\
\end{cases}\text{ on }\Omega_{f}(t),

(10)

where the Cauchy stress tensor
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{f}(\nabla\boldsymbol{u},\pi)
for the (Newtonian) fluid is given by

\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{f}(\nabla\boldsymbol{u},\pi)=2\mu\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{u})-\pi\boldsymbol{I},\qquad\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{u})=\frac{1}{2}\Big((\nabla\boldsymbol{u})+(\nabla\boldsymbol{u})^{t}\Big).

These equations are posed on the moving fluid domain
\Omega_{f}(t)
, which is determined by the Biot displacement
\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
via (
2
).

2.4
The coupling conditions

We couple the Biot and fluid subproblems via two sets of coupling conditions: a kinematic coupling condition and a dynamic coupling condition. These coupling conditions are evaluated along the moving (a priori unknown) interface
\Gamma(t)
, which introduces geometric nonlinearities into the problem, and furthermore, these coupling conditions are evaluated along an interface
\Gamma(t)
along which the fluid and Biot material
are in direct contact with each other
. We recall the definition of the Eulerian Biot velocity
\boldsymbol{\xi}
from (
8
). In addition, we define
\boldsymbol{n}(t)
and
\boldsymbol{\tau}(t)
to be the outward pointing unit normal vector and the clockwise pointing tangential vector respectively to
\Gamma(t)
.

I. Kinematic coupling conditions.
We consider two kinematic coupling conditions evaluated along the moving interface
\Gamma(t)
, one for the
continuity of the normal velocities
:

\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}(t)=(\boldsymbol{q}+\boldsymbol{\xi})\cdot\boldsymbol{n}(t),\qquad\text{ on }\Gamma(t)\text{ for all }t,

(11)

and also, the
Beavers-Joseph-Saffman condition
for the fluid tangential slip along the moving interface:

\beta(\boldsymbol{\xi}-\boldsymbol{u})\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}(t)=\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{f}(\nabla\boldsymbol{u},\pi)\boldsymbol{n}(t)\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}(t),\qquad\text{ on }\Gamma(t)\text{ for all }t.

(12)

II. Dynamic coupling conditions.
We consider two dynamic coupling conditions, one for
balance of stress
:

\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{f}(\nabla\boldsymbol{u},\pi)\boldsymbol{n}(t)=S_{b}(\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta},p)\boldsymbol{n}(t),\qquad\text{ on }\Gamma(t)\text{ for all }t,

(13)

where
S_{b}(\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta},p)
is the Piola-Kirchhoff stress tensor in Lagrangian coordinates, see (
9
), and also
balance of pressure
along the moving interface:

-\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{f}(\nabla\boldsymbol{u},\pi)\boldsymbol{n}(t)\cdot\boldsymbol{n}(t)+\frac{1}{2}|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}=p,\qquad\text{ on }\Gamma(t)\text{ for all }t.

(14)

2.5
The boundary and initial conditions

We assume that the total domain
\hat{\Omega}:=\{\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}:|\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}|<2\}
has an outer rigid wall. Therefore, we prescribe Dirichlet boundary conditions for the fluid velocity along the rigid wall:

\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{0}\qquad\text{ along }\partial\hat{\Omega}.

(15)

We also prescribe finite-energy initial data for the problem:

\boldsymbol{u}(0,\cdot)=\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in L^{2}(\Omega_{f}(0)),\quad\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(0,\cdot)=\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{0}\in H^{1}(\hat{\Omega}_{b}),\quad\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(0,\cdot)=\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{0}\in L^{2}(\hat{\Omega}_{b}),\quad\hat{p}(0,\cdot)=\hat{p}_{0}\in L^{2}(\hat{\Omega}_{b}),

with

\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{0}=0\;{\rm on}\;\Omega_{f}(0),\quad\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}(0)=0\;{\rm on}\;\partial\hat{\Omega}.

(16)

2.6
A discussion of past work in FPSI

The study of fluid-poroelastic structure interaction (FPSI) is a mathematical area of recent interest, featuring applications to physical and biological sciences, which has been studied through a variety of mathematical approaches: analytic, numerical, and modeling. FPSI has grown out of the broader and classical study of the dynamical interaction between incompressible fluids and deformable elastic structures, known as fluid-structure interaction (FSI).

The study of FSI began with fluid-structure models with
linear geometric coupling
, in which the fluid and structure domains (although moving in practice) are fixed for the purposes of the model, and the fluid/structure equations are hence posed on fixed domains
[
6
,
7
,
50
]
, as a model for small displacements. The study of FSI expanded to more complex models of fluid-structure dynamics with
nonlinear geometric coupling
, involving a priori unknown and time-dependent moving fluid and structure domains, which introduce additional geometric nonlinearities and significant mathematical difficulties into the mathematical analysis. These types of moving boundary models with nonlinear geometric coupling have been analyzed through a variety of approaches, including strong solution approaches
[
11
,
52
,
39
,
40
]
and weak solution approaches
[
28
,
38
,
57
,
51
,
40
]
to models involving incompressible fluids occupying domains with elastic boundaries of lower dimensionality. In addition, models involving immersed elastic solids and deformable solids of the same dimension as the fluid domain have been analyzed in
[
12
,
20
,
32
,
33
,
29
,
30
,
49
,
43
,
44
,
66
]
.

Poroelasticity has been of interest since the mid-1900s, when Biot proposed the
Biot equations
of poroelasticity for modeling poroelastic materials, in the context of geosciences and soil consolidation
[
13
,
14
]
. Since then, the use of the Biot equations of poroelasticity have expanded into a much broader range of real-life applications, including the modeling of fractures and groundwater flows
[
37
,
53
]
and the modeling of biological tissues by poroelasticity in biomedical applications
[
76
,
23
,
26
,
77
]
. The practical importance of poroelasticity and the abundance of poroelastic materials in science have motivated the rigorous mathematical analysis of the Biot equations, and by now, there has been extensive work on studying well-posedness for the Biot equations via rigorous mathematical PDE analysis
[
8
,
9
,
10
,
64
,
67
,
69
,
74
,
15
,
19
,
16
,
17
]
.

Motivated in particular by applications to problems in which poroelastic media interacts with an elastic or deformable solid, the study of fluid-poroelastic structure interaction has been a recent field of mathematical research, which inherits many of the mathematical difficulties (including complex coupling along interfaces, moving domains, geometric nonlinearities) of FSI problems at large and the mathematical challenges associated with the analysis of the Biot equations. FPSI models with linear geometric coupling (with equations posed on fixed interfaces) were studied in works such as
[
2
,
27
,
68
]
, and a FPSI model with linear geometric coupling and a poroelastic plate at the fluid-poroelastic material interface was considered in
[
18
]
, arising for a need for a rigorous mathematical model for bioartificial organs in biomedical applications
[
76
]
.

In
[
18
]
, the poroelastic plate exists at the interface, separating the fluid domain and the Biot domain containing the poroelastic material. The use of plates at the fluid-structure interface is physically motivated, since many biological tissues are multilayered with both thick elastic layers and thin elastic layers, which has already been observed for example in FSI applications
[
59
,
75
,
72
,
18
]
. However, in addition as has been proposed in
[
59
]
in the broader context of FSI, the thin layer at the interface between the thick elastic structure and fluid can serve as a regularizing mechanism for the interface dynamics, as the trace of the elastic structure displacement along the interface at the level of weak solutions does not have sufficient regularity to interpret the interface as a continuous well-defined interface, without additional regularity in the elastodynamics of the structure.

The idea of using a thin elastic plate to regularize interface dynamics in moving boundary FSI problems was applied to FPSI in
[
47
,
48
]
, where existence of a weak solution to a regularized weak formulation was achieved for a moving boundary FPSI model with nonlinear geometric coupling and a reticular plate at the Biot-fluid interface. The regularized weak formulation in this case involved regularizing terms in the weak formulation related to the geometry of the Lagrangian map, and it is shown in
[
48
]
that this regularization is consistent with classical solutions to the original non-regularized problem when such classical solutions exist, in the sense that these regularized solutions converge as the regularization parameter goes to zero, which is called
weak-classical consistency
. This motivates the approach of a regularized interface solution used in the current manuscript, though it is crucial to note that the interface itself is not regularized in
[
48
]
because of the elastodynamics of the reticular plate, which already gives the interface displacement a regularity of
H^{2}_{0}(\Gamma)
. However, in the current model with direct contact, we use a regularized interface condition, where the interface displacement is
\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\delta}|_{\Gamma}
, given by a spatial convoluted version of
\boldsymbol{\eta}
, rather than
\boldsymbol{\eta}|_{\Gamma}
, since there is no plate at the interface to serve as a regularizing mechanism for the interface dynamics.

Such a direct contact problem between a Biot medium and a fluid is classical and of both practical and mathematical importance, as many mathematical well-posedness results for the Biot-fluid problem are direct contact problems. In the literature, we mention in particular the recent works
[
3
,
4
]
, which study well-posedness for a direct contact Biot-fluid problem with a Beavers-Joseph-Saffman condition imposed along the (linearized) direct contact interface and
linear geometric coupling
. In particular, this work establishes existence of strong solutions satisfying an energy identity and weak solutions satisfying an energy inequality for appropriate initial data and source terms, via a semigroup approach.
The work in this manuscript extends work on direct contact Biot-fluid FPSI problems by providing, to the best of our knowledge, the first rigorous well-posedness result for a direct contact Biot/fluid FPSI problem with nonlinear geometric coupling/a priori unknown moving domains, hence representing a significant development in the field of FPSI.
We remark that such direct contact FPSI models are essential in applications, as shown by the wealth of mathematical literature on the numerical simulation of such models for example in the work
[
5
]
, by mixed formulation approaches
[
55
,
25
,
54
,
24
]
and loosely coupled partitioned schemes
[
21
,
65
]
. As such, the current manuscript addresses a relevant problem of particular importance to the broader FSI/FPSI mathematical and engineering communities.

Our approach relies fundamentally on a constructive existence scheme given by Lie operator splitting, first proposed in
[
57
]
, in which the full multiphysical FSI/FPSI problem is split into separate subproblems in a modular approach. This is a robust approach to analyzing FSI and FPSI problems both analytically in terms of well-posedness of FSI models via a constructive existence approach
[
57
,
58
,
59
,
60
,
61
,
18
,
70
,
56
]
and numerically especially for FPSI models in
[
22
,
21
,
65
]
. We use this operator splitting approach to show constructive existence for an approximate problem that approximates the direct contact problem we study, which splits the interface displacement from the Biot/fluid dynamics.

Second, our approach builds on the regularized weak formulation framework developed in
[
47
,
48
]
for problems involving bulk (poro)elasticity and a thin plate at the interface, transparent to fluid flow, and extends it by introducing a novel
regularized interface method
that enables the analysis of
direct contact problems
with nonlinear geometric coupling and bulk elasticity. We believe the regularized interface method proposed here is broadly applicable, including to FSI models with bulk elasticity and to nonlinear elastic materials in three spatial dimensions. In particular, this approach is well-suited for studying problems with
vector-valued elastic displacements in bulk elastic materials
within the weak solutions framework—a significant advance, as most existing works on weak solutions for FSI models focus on scalar displacements. Exceptions include
[
73
,
41
,
45
,
61
,
72
,
12
]
, which consider vector-valued displacements for elastic plates or shells (rather than bulk materials), with
[
41
,
45
,
61
]
addressing 2D fluids coupled to 1D structures, penalization of domain degeneracy via compression in
[
45
]
, and additional a priori assumptions on Lipschitz domains in
[
72
]
. Moreover, in
[
12
,
20
]
, weak solutions to FSI problems with bulk
3D
viscoelasticity were obtained, but only for higher-order viscoelasticity models. While our exposition focuses on a 2D/2D bulk elasticity problem, we expect the regularized interface method to be robust for FSI problems with vector-valued displacements in higher dimensions, and we introduce new techniques for controlling geometric degeneracies in bulk elastic materials with vector-valued displacements.

3
Definition of the weak formulation

3.1
Weak formulation on the physical domain

We first define an appropriate weak formulation for our nonlinearly geometrically coupled FPSI problem with direct contact on the physical domain by testing against sufficiently regular test functions. We derive the weak formulation for the full problem as follows.

Fluid momentum equation.
We test the momentum equation in the fluid equation (
10
) by a fluid test function
\boldsymbol{v}
by multiplying by
\boldsymbol{v}
and then integrating over
\Omega_{f}(t)
. We obtain by using the Reynold’s transport theorem and integration by parts:

\int_{\Omega_{f}(t)}(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}+(\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{u})-(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{f}(\nabla\boldsymbol{u},\pi)))\cdot\boldsymbol{v}=\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\Omega_{f}(t)}\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}-\int_{\Omega_{f}(t)}\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{v}+2\nu\int_{\Omega_{f}(t)}\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{u}):\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{v})\\
+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega_{f}(t)}[((\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{u})\cdot\boldsymbol{v}-((\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{v})\cdot\boldsymbol{u}]+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Gamma(t)}(\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}-2\boldsymbol{\xi}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}-\int_{\Gamma(t)}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{f}(\nabla\boldsymbol{u},\pi)\boldsymbol{n}\cdot\boldsymbol{v},

(17)

where
\boldsymbol{\xi}
is the Eulerian structure velocity defined in (
8
).

Biot elasticity equation.
We test the first elastodynamics equation in the Biot equations (
4
) by a test function
\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}
and obtain by integration by parts, by using properties of the Piola transform, and by using the definition of the viscoelastic Piola-Kirchhoff stress tensor in (
5
):

\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}(\rho_{b}\partial_{tt}\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}-\hat{\nabla}\cdot\hat{S}_{b}(\hat{\nabla}\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}},\hat{p})\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=\rho_{b}\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}-\rho_{b}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\cdot\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}-\alpha\int_{\Omega_{b}(t)}p(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi})\\
+\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}(2\mu_{e}\hat{D}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}):\hat{\boldsymbol{D}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}})+\lambda_{e}(\hat{\nabla}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}})(\hat{\nabla}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}})+2\mu_{v}\hat{\boldsymbol{D}}(\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}):\hat{\boldsymbol{D}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}})+\lambda_{v}(\hat{\nabla}\cdot\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}})(\nabla\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}})+\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}\hat{S}_{b}(\hat{\nabla}\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}},\hat{p})\boldsymbol{e}_{r}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}},

(18)

where
p=\hat{p}\circ(\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\eta}_{b})^{-1}
and
\boldsymbol{\psi}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\circ(\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\eta}_{b})^{-1}
.

Biot pore pressure equation.
We multiply the second pore pressure equation in the Biot equations (
4
) by a pore pressure test function
r
and integrate by parts and use a change of variables via the Lagrangian map
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\eta}_{b}
, recalling that the Darcy velocity
\boldsymbol{q}
is defined as
\boldsymbol{q}:=-\kappa\nabla p
on
\Omega_{b}(t)
:

\int_{\Omega_{b}(t)}\left(\frac{c_{0}}{[\text{det}(\hat{\nabla}\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\eta}_{b})]\circ(\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\eta}_{b})^{-1}}\frac{D}{Dt}p+\alpha\nabla\cdot\frac{D}{Dt}\boldsymbol{\eta}-\nabla\cdot(\kappa\nabla p)\right)r\\
=c_{0}\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}\hat{p}\cdot\hat{r}-c_{0}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}\hat{p}\cdot\partial_{t}\hat{r}-\alpha\int_{\Omega_{b}(t)}\frac{D}{Dt}\boldsymbol{\eta}\cdot\nabla r+\kappa\int_{\Omega_{b}(t)}\nabla p\cdot\nabla r-\alpha\int_{\Gamma(t)}(\boldsymbol{\xi}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})r-\int_{\Gamma(t)}(\boldsymbol{q}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})r.

(19)

Conclusion of the derivation.
To obtain the final weak formulation, we add together the results of (
17
), (
18
), and (
19
). Since the filtration velocity
\boldsymbol{q}
is not explicitly solved for in a weak solution, we use the continuity of velocity condition in (
11
) to rewrite the term
\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma(t)}(\boldsymbol{q}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})r
in (
19
) as

\int_{\Gamma(t)}(\boldsymbol{q}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})r=\int_{\Gamma(t)}((\boldsymbol{u}-\boldsymbol{\xi})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})r.

In addition, we can combine the boundary terms arising from the fluid and Biot stress tensors as follows. By the continuity of normal stresses in (
13
), we obtain that

\displaystyle-\int_{\Gamma(t)}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{f}(\nabla\boldsymbol{u},\pi)\boldsymbol{n}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}

\displaystyle+\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}\hat{S}_{b}(\hat{\nabla}\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}},\hat{p})\boldsymbol{e}_{r}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=\int_{\Gamma(t)}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{f}(\nabla\boldsymbol{u},\pi)\boldsymbol{n}\cdot(\boldsymbol{\psi}-\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle=\int_{\Gamma(t)}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{f}(\nabla\boldsymbol{u},\pi)\boldsymbol{n}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}[(\boldsymbol{\psi}-\boldsymbol{v})\cdot\boldsymbol{n}]+\int_{\Gamma(t)}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{f}(\nabla\boldsymbol{u},\pi)\boldsymbol{n}\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}[(\boldsymbol{\psi}-\boldsymbol{v})\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}]

\displaystyle=\int_{\Gamma(t)}\left(\frac{1}{2}|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}-p\right)(\boldsymbol{\psi}-\boldsymbol{v})\cdot\boldsymbol{n}+\int_{\Gamma(t)}\beta(\boldsymbol{\xi}-\boldsymbol{u})\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}(\boldsymbol{\psi}-\boldsymbol{v})\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau},

where we used the coupling conditions (
12
) and (
14
) in the last step. We therefore, after integrating from
t=0
to
t=T
in the time variable, have obtained the following weak formulation for the direct contact FPSI problem with nonlinear geometric coupling.

Definition 3.1

(Weak solution, moving domain formulation)
.

We say that
(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\eta},p)
is a weak solution to the FPSI problem if for all
C_{c}^{1}([0,T))
(continuously differentiable) test functions
(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{\psi},r)
taking values in an appropriate test space with
\boldsymbol{v}
defined on
\Omega_{f}(t)
, and both
\boldsymbol{\psi}
and
r
defined on
\Omega_{b}(t)
, the following weak formulation holds:

-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{f}(t)}\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{v}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{f}(t)}[((\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{u})\cdot\boldsymbol{v}-((\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{v})\cdot\boldsymbol{u}]+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma(t)}(\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}-2\boldsymbol{\xi}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}\\
+2\mu\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{f}(t)}\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{u}):\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{v})+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma(t)}\left(\frac{1}{2}|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}-p\right)(\boldsymbol{\psi}-\boldsymbol{v})\cdot\boldsymbol{n}(t)+\beta\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma}(\boldsymbol{\xi}-\boldsymbol{u})\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}(\boldsymbol{\psi}-\boldsymbol{v})\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}\\
-\rho_{b}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\cdot\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}+2\mu_{e}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}\hat{\boldsymbol{D}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}):\hat{\boldsymbol{D}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}})+\lambda_{e}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}(\hat{\nabla}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}})(\hat{\nabla}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}})+2\mu_{v}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}\hat{\boldsymbol{D}}(\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}):\hat{\boldsymbol{D}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}})\\
+\lambda_{v}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}(\hat{\nabla}\cdot\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}})(\hat{\nabla}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}})-\alpha\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{b}(t)}p\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}-c_{0}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}\hat{p}\cdot\partial_{t}\hat{r}\\
-\alpha\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{b}(t)}\frac{D}{Dt}\boldsymbol{\eta}\cdot\nabla r-\alpha\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma(t)}(\boldsymbol{\xi}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})r+\kappa\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{b}(t)}\nabla p\cdot\nabla r-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma(t)}((\boldsymbol{u}-\boldsymbol{\xi})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})r\\
=\int_{\Omega_{f}(0)}\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}(0)+\rho_{b}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{0}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}(0)+c_{0}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}\hat{p}_{0}\cdot\hat{r}(0).

3.2
Transformations between fixed and moving domains

Our next goal is to define an appropriate weak formulation on the fixed domain, to give a weak formulation in the fixed domain (Lagrangian) formulation. This will involve examining maps between fixed domains and moving (physical) domains, and examining how differential operators and functions transform between the fixed and moving domains.

Transformation between the fixed and moving Biot domains.
We have the Lagrangian map
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\eta}_{b}:\hat{\Omega}_{b}\to\Omega_{b}(t)
, defined in (
3
). Given a scalar function
g:\Omega_{b}(t)\to{\mathbb{R}}
or a vector-valued function
\boldsymbol{g}:\Omega_{b}(t)\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, we can define corresponding transformed functions on the fixed domain via:

\hat{g}(\hat{x},\hat{y}):=g(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\eta}_{b}(\hat{x},\hat{y})),\qquad\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}(\hat{x},\hat{y}):=\boldsymbol{g}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\eta}_{b}(\hat{x},\hat{y})),\qquad\text{ for }(\hat{x},\hat{y})\in\hat{\Omega}_{b}.

(20)

Furthermore, by the chain rule, we have the following formula for the transformation of spatial gradients via the Lagrangian map:

\hat{\nabla}^{\eta}_{b}\hat{g}=(\nabla g)\circ\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\eta}_{b},\qquad\text{ where }\hat{\nabla}^{\eta}_{b}\hat{g}=\left(\frac{\partial\hat{g}}{\partial\hat{x}},\frac{\partial\hat{g}}{\partial\hat{y}}\right)\cdot(\boldsymbol{I}+\hat{\nabla}\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}})^{-1}.

(21)

To clarify notation,
\displaystyle\hat{\nabla}\hat{g}:=\left(\frac{\partial\hat{g}}{\partial\hat{x}},\frac{\partial\hat{g}}{\partial\hat{y}}\right)
is the (standard) gradient on the fixed domain
\hat{\Omega}_{b}
,
\displaystyle\nabla g:=\left(\frac{\partial g}{\partial x},\frac{\partial g}{\partial y}\right)
is the (standard) gradient on the moving domain
\Omega_{b}(t)
, and
\hat{\nabla}^{\eta}_{b}
is the transformed gradient on the reference domain
\hat{\Omega}_{b}
. The Jacobian of the Lagrangian map will be denoted by

\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{\eta}_{b}:=\text{det}(\boldsymbol{I}+\hat{\nabla}\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}).

(22)

Transformation between the fixed and moving fluid domains.
Given the vector-valued interface displacement
\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}|_{\hat{\Gamma}}
, we will define an associated
Arbitrary Lagrangian-Eulerian (ALE) map

\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\eta}_{f}:\hat{\Omega}_{f}\to\Omega_{f}(t)
. Because
\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}|_{\hat{\Gamma}}
is a two-dimensional vector field along
\hat{\Gamma}
, we use the approach of
harmonic extension
to define the ALE map. Namely, we define
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\eta}_{f}:\overline{\hat{\Omega}_{f}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
to be the unique solution to the following elliptic problem:

\Delta\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\eta}_{f}=\boldsymbol{0}\qquad\text{ on }\hat{\Omega}_{f},

(23)

with boundary conditions

\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\eta}_{f}(\hat{x},\hat{y})=(\hat{x},\hat{y})+\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}|_{\hat{\Gamma}}(\hat{x},\hat{y})\text{ on }\hat{\Gamma},\qquad\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\eta}_{f}(\hat{x},\hat{y})=(\hat{x},\hat{y})\text{ on }\partial\hat{\Omega}_{f}\setminus\hat{\Gamma}.

(24)

By elliptic regularity theorems and Sobolev embedding (see
[
42
]
and the discussion preceding Proposition 2.1 in
[
73
]
), we have that for all
p\geq 2
,

\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\eta}_{f}\|_{W^{2,p}(\hat{\Omega}_{f})}\leq C_{p}\|(\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}|_{\hat{\Gamma}})\|_{W^{2-\frac{1}{p},p}(\hat{\Gamma})}\leq C_{p}\|(\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}|_{\hat{\Gamma}})\|_{H^{\frac{5}{2}-\frac{2}{p}}(\hat{\Gamma})}.

We then define the Jacobian of the ALE mapping
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\eta}_{f}:\hat{\Omega}_{f}\to\Omega_{f}(t)
by

\mathcal{J}^{\eta}_{f}:=\text{det}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\eta}_{f})

and we define the associated velocity of the ALE map defined on the reference domain
\hat{\Omega}_{f}
:

\hat{\boldsymbol{w}}^{\eta}(\hat{x},\hat{y})=\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\eta}_{f}(\hat{x},\hat{y}),\qquad\text{ for }(\hat{x},\hat{y})\in\hat{\Omega}_{f}.

(25)

We can use the ALE map to transform functions from the moving (physical) domain to the reference domain: given a function
h
defined on
\Omega_{f}(t)
, we can define

\hat{h}(\hat{x},\hat{y}):=h(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\eta}_{f}(\hat{x},\hat{y})),\qquad\text{ for }(\hat{x},\hat{y})\in\hat{\Omega}_{f},

(26)

and by the chain rule, we have the following transformation rule for spatial derivatives:

\hat{\nabla}^{\eta}_{f}\hat{h}=(\nabla h)\circ\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\eta}_{f}\quad\text{ where }\hat{\nabla}^{\eta}_{f}\hat{h}:=\left(\frac{\partial\hat{h}}{\partial\hat{x}},\frac{\partial\hat{h}}{\partial\hat{y}}\right)\cdot\Big(\hat{\nabla}\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\eta}_{f}\Big)^{-1},

(27)

analogously to (
21
), and by taking traces, we can define the transformed divergence:

\hat{\nabla}^{\eta}_{f}\cdot\hat{h}=(\nabla\cdot h)\circ\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\omega}_{f}\quad\text{ where }\hat{\nabla}^{\eta}_{f}\cdot\hat{h}:=\text{tr}\left[\left(\frac{\partial\hat{h}}{\partial\hat{x}},\frac{\partial\hat{h}}{\partial\hat{y}}\right)\cdot\Big(\hat{\nabla}\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\eta}_{f}\Big)^{-1}\right].

(28)

We also have the following transformation rule for time derivatives;

\partial_{t}h(\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\eta}_{f}(\hat{x},\hat{y}))=\partial_{t}\hat{h}(\hat{x},\hat{y})-(\hat{\boldsymbol{w}}^{\eta}\cdot\hat{\nabla}^{\eta}_{f})\hat{h}(\hat{x},\hat{y}).

(29)

More generally, given a (vector-valued) function
\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}:\hat{\Gamma}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, we define the associated fluid ALE map
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\omega}_{f}:\hat{\Omega}_{f}\to\Omega_{f}(t)
as the solution to

\Delta\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\omega}_{f}=\boldsymbol{0}\qquad\text{ on }\hat{\Omega}_{f},

(30)

with boundary conditions

\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\omega}_{f}(\hat{x},\hat{y})=(\hat{x},\hat{y})+\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}(\hat{x},\hat{y})\text{ on }\hat{\Gamma},\qquad\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\omega}_{f}(\hat{x},\hat{y})=(\hat{x},\hat{y})\text{ on }\partial\hat{\Omega}_{f}\setminus\hat{\Gamma}.

(31)

In this context, we have the analogous definitions of

\hat{h}(\hat{x},\hat{y}):=h(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\omega}_{f}(\hat{x},\hat{y})),\qquad\hat{\nabla}^{\omega}_{f}\hat{h}=(\nabla h)\circ\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\omega}_{f}\quad\text{ where }\hat{\nabla}^{\omega}_{f}\hat{h}:=\left(\frac{\partial\hat{h}}{\partial\hat{x}},\frac{\partial\hat{h}}{\partial\hat{y}}\right)\cdot\left(\hat{\nabla}\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\omega}_{f}\right)^{-1},

\hat{\nabla}^{\omega}_{f}\cdot\hat{h}=(\nabla\cdot h)\circ\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\omega}_{f}\quad\text{ where }\hat{\nabla}^{\omega}_{f}\cdot\hat{h}:=\text{tr}\left[\left(\frac{\partial\hat{h}}{\partial\hat{x}},\frac{\partial\hat{h}}{\partial\hat{y}}\right)\cdot\left(\hat{\nabla}\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\omega}_{f}\right)^{-1}\right].

(32)

We can also define the ALE velocity
\hat{\boldsymbol{w}}^{\omega}=\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\omega}_{f}
and we obtain a similar transformation rule for time derivatives as in (
29
).

Remark 3.1

(Notation remarks)
.

Using the more general notational convention for the ALE map in (
30
) and (
31
) given a vector-valued interface displacement
\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}
, the definition of the ALE map defined the Biot displacement
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\eta}_{f}
given in (
23
) and (
24
) is more accurately described by
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\eta|_{\Gamma}}_{f}
, since the interface displacement is given by
\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}|_{\hat{\Gamma}}
in the direct contact fluid-Biot problem. However, we choose to use the notation
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\eta}_{f}
for notational simplicity.

As a notational remark, note that we use the same notation for functions pulled back to the reference domain, for functions defined on the Biot domain via the Biot Lagrangian map
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\eta}_{b}
in (
20
) and for functions defined on the fluid domain via the ALE fluid map
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\eta}_{f}
in (
26
). While we do not explicitly disambiguate the notation as both are denoted using the same “hat” notation
\hat{h}
for example, we rely on context to disambiguate these two notions, as it will be clear from the context whether the associated function is defined on the Biot domain or the fluid domain.

Transformation between the fixed and moving interface.
Next, we describe the transformation from the fixed reference interface
\hat{\Gamma}
and the moving interface
\Gamma(t)
. To do this, we note that it will be convenient to use a standard parametrization to parametrize the unit circle
\hat{\Gamma}
:

\hat{z}\in[0,2\pi]\to(\cos(\hat{z}),\sin(\hat{z}))\in\hat{\Gamma}.

(33)

Hence, given a (scalar-valued or vector-valued) function
\hat{f}(\hat{x},\hat{y})
for
(\hat{x},\hat{y})\in\hat{\Gamma}
, we can regard the function as a periodic function on
[0,2\pi]
as:

\hat{f}(\hat{z}):=\hat{f}(\cos(\hat{z}),\sin(\hat{z})),\qquad\text{ for }\hat{z}\in[0,2\pi].

To simplify notation, we will implicitly switch between the notations
\hat{f}(\hat{z})
and
\hat{f}(\hat{x},\hat{y})
for functions
\hat{f}
defined on
\hat{\Gamma}
, without explicitly notating the composition of the function with the standard parametrization (
33
).

Next, we define the transformation between
\hat{\Gamma}
and
\Gamma(t)
, described by the vector-valued interface displacement
\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}|_{\hat{\Gamma}}:\hat{\Gamma}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, denoted component-wise as
\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}|_{\hat{\Gamma}}(\hat{z}):=(\hat{\eta}_{1}|_{\hat{\Gamma}}(\hat{z}),\hat{\eta}_{2}|_{\hat{\Gamma}}(\hat{z}))
,
\hat{z}\in[0,2\pi]
, for the (vector-valued) Biot displacement
\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}=(\hat{\eta}_{1},\hat{\eta}_{2})
. We define the map
\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\eta}_{\Gamma}:\hat{\Gamma}\to\Gamma(t)
defined by

\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\eta}_{\Gamma}(\hat{z}):=(\cos(\hat{z}),\sin(\hat{z}))+\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}|_{\hat{\Gamma}}(\hat{z})=(\cos(\hat{z})+\hat{\eta}_{1}|_{\hat{\Gamma}}(\hat{z}),\sin(\hat{z})+\hat{\eta}_{2}|_{\hat{\Gamma}}(\hat{z})).

(34)

In addition, we define some relevant quantities related to the geometry of the moving interface
\Gamma(t)
. We first define the arc length element, given by

\hat{\mathcal{S}}^{\eta}_{\Gamma}:=\sqrt{(-\sin(\hat{z})+\partial_{\hat{z}}\hat{\eta}_{1}|_{\hat{\Gamma}})^{2}+(\cos(\hat{z})+\partial_{\hat{z}}\hat{\eta}_{2}|_{\hat{\Gamma}})^{2}},

(35)

and we define the normal vector
\boldsymbol{n}
and the tangent vector
\boldsymbol{\tau}
to the moving interface
\Gamma(t)
(with a standardized orientation) by

\boldsymbol{n}:=\frac{1}{\hat{S}^{\eta}_{\Gamma}}(\cos(\hat{z})+\partial_{\hat{z}}\hat{\eta}_{2}|_{\hat{\Gamma}},\sin(\hat{z})-\partial_{\hat{z}}\hat{\eta}_{1}|_{\hat{\Gamma}}),\qquad\boldsymbol{\tau}:=\frac{1}{\hat{S}^{\eta}_{\Gamma}}(-\sin(\hat{z})+\partial_{\hat{z}}\hat{\eta}_{1}|_{\hat{\Gamma}},\cos(\hat{z})+\partial_{\hat{z}}\hat{\eta}_{2}|_{\hat{\Gamma}}).

(36)

As for the fluid ALE map in (
30
) and (
31
), it will at times be more convenient to instead emphasize the dependence of the Lagrangian map for the interface by its vector-valued displacement
\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}
in which case, the notation is analogous:

\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\omega}_{\Gamma}(\hat{z}):=(\cos(\hat{z}),\sin(\hat{z}))+\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}(\hat{z})=(\cos(\hat{z})+\hat{\omega}_{1}(\hat{z}),\sin(\hat{z})+\hat{\omega}_{2}(\hat{z}))\quad\text{ for }\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\omega}_{\Gamma}:\hat{\Gamma}\to\Gamma(t),

(37)

\hat{\mathcal{S}}^{\omega}_{\Gamma}:=\sqrt{(-\sin(\hat{z})+\partial_{\hat{z}}\hat{\omega}_{1})^{2}+(\cos(\hat{z})+\partial_{\hat{z}}\hat{\omega}_{2})^{2}},

\boldsymbol{n}:=\frac{1}{\hat{S}^{\omega}_{\Gamma}}(\cos(\hat{z})+\partial_{\hat{z}}\hat{\omega}_{2},\sin(\hat{z})-\partial_{\hat{z}}\hat{\omega}_{1}),\quad\boldsymbol{\tau}:=\frac{1}{\hat{\mathcal{S}}^{\omega}_{\Gamma}}(-\sin(\hat{z})+\partial_{\hat{z}}\hat{\omega}_{1},\cos(\hat{z})+\partial_{\hat{z}}\hat{\omega}_{2}).

(38)

Some results about injectivity.
We observe that these transformations between fixed and moving domains that we have defined for the fluid, the Biot medium, and the fluid-Biot interface, are only well-defined when the maps are injective, and therefore, it will be useful to have ways of characterizing injectivity. We start with the following result about injectivity of the map for the moving interface.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}^{*}:[0,2\pi]\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
be a continuously differentiable
C^{1}(\hat{\Gamma})
function with
\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}^{*}(0)=\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}^{*}(2\pi)
, which satisfies the following non-degeneracy condition for some positive constant
\alpha>0
:

|(-\sin(\hat{z}),\cos(\hat{z}))+\partial_{\hat{z}}\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}^{*}(\hat{z})|\geq\alpha\text{ for all }\hat{z}\in[0,2\pi],

and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\omega^{*}}_{\Gamma}(\hat{z}):=(\cos(\hat{z}),\sin(\hat{z}))+\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}^{*}(\hat{z})
is an injective map on
\hat{\Gamma}
such that
\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\omega^{*}}_{\Gamma}(\hat{\Gamma})\cap\partial\hat{\Omega}=\varnothing
for
\hat{\Omega}:=\{\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}:|\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}|<2\}
. Then, there exists
\delta>0
sufficiently small such that for all
C^{1}(\hat{\Gamma})
functions
\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}:[0,2\pi]\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
such that

\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}(0)=\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}(2\pi)\quad\text{ and }\quad\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}^{*}\|_{C^{1}(\hat{\Gamma})}\leq\delta,

the map
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\omega}_{\Gamma}(\hat{z}):=(\cos(\hat{z}),\sin(\hat{z}))+\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}(\hat{z})
is injective and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\omega}_{\Gamma}(\hat{\Gamma})\cap\partial\hat{\Omega}=\varnothing
, with

\|(-\sin(\hat{z}),\cos(\hat{z}))+\partial_{\hat{z}}\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}(\hat{z})\|\geq\frac{\alpha}{2}\text{ for all }\hat{z}\in[0,2\pi].

(39)

Proof.

Consider the following (vector-valued) function
f:[0,2\pi]\times[0,2\pi]\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, defined by

f^{\omega}(z_{1},z_{2})=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\omega}_{\Gamma}(\hat{z}_{1})-\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\omega}_{\Gamma}(\hat{z}_{2})}{|z_{1}-z_{2}|},\quad&\text{ if }\hat{z}_{1}\neq\hat{z}_{2},\\
(-\sin(\hat{z}),\cos(\hat{z}))+\partial_{\hat{z}}\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}(\hat{z}),\quad&\text{ if }\hat{z}_{1}=\hat{z}_{2}=\hat{z}.\\
\end{cases}

Note that by the given assumptions on
\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}^{*}
, we have that
|f^{\omega^{*}}(\hat{z}_{1},\hat{z}_{2})|>0
for all
\hat{z}_{1},\hat{z}_{2}\in[0,2\pi]
, and since
\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}^{*}
is continuously differentiable,
f^{\omega^{*}}(\hat{z}_{1},\hat{z}_{2})
is continuous on
[0,2\pi]\times[0,2\pi]
. So there exists some positive constant
c
such that
f^{\omega^{*}}(\hat{z}_{1},\hat{z}_{2})\geq c>0
for
\hat{z}_{1},\hat{z}_{2}\in[0,2\pi]
. For
\hat{z}_{1}\neq\hat{z}_{2}
,

\displaystyle|f^{\omega}(\hat{z}_{1},\hat{z}_{2})-f^{\omega^{*}}(\hat{z}_{1},\hat{z}_{2})|

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\omega}_{\Gamma}(\hat{z}_{1})-\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\omega}_{\Gamma}(\hat{z}_{2})}{|\hat{z}_{1}-\hat{z}_{2}|}-\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\omega^{*}}_{\Gamma}(\hat{z}_{1})-\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\omega^{*}}_{\Gamma}(\hat{z}_{2})}{|\hat{z}_{1}-\hat{z}_{2}|}\right|

\displaystyle=\frac{|(\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}^{*})(\hat{z}_{1})-(\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}^{*})(\hat{z}_{2})|}{|\hat{z}_{1}-\hat{z}_{2}|}\leq\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}^{*}\|_{C^{1}(\hat{\Gamma})}.

Therefore, there exists
\delta>0
sufficiently small such that for all
\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}
with
\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}(0)=\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}(2\pi)
and
\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}^{*}\|_{C^{1}(\hat{\Gamma})}\leq\delta
, we have that
f^{\omega}(\hat{z}_{1},\hat{z}_{2})\geq c/2>0
for all
\hat{z}_{1},\hat{z}_{2}\in[0,L]
, which implies that
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\omega}_{\Gamma}
is injective. Finally, to show that
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\omega}_{\Gamma}(\hat{\Gamma})\cap\partial\hat{\Omega}=\varnothing
for
\delta
sufficiently small, note that
\text{dist}\Big(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\omega^{*}}_{\Gamma}(\hat{\Gamma}),\partial\hat{\Omega}\Big)\geq c>0
for some positive constant
c
, so for
\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}^{*}\|_{C^{1}(\hat{\Gamma})}\leq\delta
for
\delta<c/2
, we have that
\text{dist}\Big(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\omega}_{\Gamma}(\hat{\Gamma}),\partial\hat{\Omega}\Big)\geq c/2
. The non-degeneracy claim in (
39
) follows similarly.

∎

Proposition 3.2
.

Consider a
C^{1}(\hat{\Gamma})
function
\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}:[0,2\pi]\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
such that
\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}(0)=\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}(2\pi)
,
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\omega}_{f}
is injective on
[0,L]
,
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\omega}_{f}(\hat{\Gamma})\cap\partial\hat{\Omega}=\varnothing
, and (
39
) is satisfied for some positive constant
\alpha>0
.

•

If
\text{det}(\boldsymbol{I}+\nabla\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}})>0
on
\hat{\Omega}_{b}
for some
C^{1}
function
\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
on
\hat{\overline{\Omega}}_{b}
, then
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\eta}_{b}
is a bijective map from
\hat{\Omega}_{b}
to
\Omega_{b}(t)
.

•

If
\text{det}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\omega}_{f})>0
on
\hat{\Omega}_{f}
where
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\omega}_{f}
is
C^{1}
on
\hat{\overline{\Omega}}_{f}
, then
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\omega}_{f}
is a bijective map from
\hat{\Omega}_{f}
to
\Omega_{f}(t)
.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of Theorem 5-5-2 in
[
31
]
and the previous proposition.
∎

3.3
Weak formulation on the fixed domain

Using the function transformations defined in the previous section, we can consider the weak formulation to the direct contact FPSI problem on the fixed domain. For this purpose, we introduce the rescaled normal and tangent vectors, compare with the unit normal and tangent vectors defined in (
36
), as follows:

\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}^{\eta}:=(\cos(\hat{z})+\partial_{\hat{z}}\hat{\eta}_{2}|_{\hat{\Gamma}},\sin(\hat{z})-\partial_{\hat{z}}\hat{\eta}_{1}|_{\hat{\Gamma}}),\qquad\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\eta}=(-\sin(\hat{z})+\partial_{\hat{z}}\hat{\eta}_{1}|_{\hat{\Gamma}},\cos(\hat{z})+\partial_{\hat{z}}\hat{\eta}_{2}|_{\hat{\Gamma}}),

(40)

and we also recall the definition of the arc length element from (
35
). Using the maps from the reference to moving domains for the Biot medium, the fluid domain, and the plate, we thus have the following definition of a weak solution to the FPSI problem on the reference domain.

Definition 3.2

(Weak formulation, fixed domain formulation)
.

We say that
(\hat{\boldsymbol{u}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}},\hat{p})
is a weak solution to the FPSI problem (on the fixed domain) if for all
C_{c}^{1}([0,T))
(continuously differentiable) test functions
(\hat{\boldsymbol{v}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}},\hat{r})
for the fluid velocity, Biot displacement, and pore pressure respectively, taking values in an appropriate test space, the following weak formulation holds:

-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{f}}\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{\eta}_{f}\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{v}}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{f}}\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{\eta}_{f}\Big[((\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}-\hat{\boldsymbol{w}}^{\eta})\cdot\hat{\nabla}^{\eta}_{f}\hat{\boldsymbol{u}})\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{v}}-((\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}-\hat{\boldsymbol{w}}^{\eta})\cdot\hat{\nabla}^{\eta}_{f}\hat{\boldsymbol{v}})\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}\Big]\\
-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{f}}(\partial_{t}\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{\eta}_{f})\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{v}}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}})\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}^{\eta}(\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{v}})+2\nu\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{f}}\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{\eta}_{f}\hat{\boldsymbol{D}}^{\eta}_{f}(\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}):\hat{\boldsymbol{D}}^{\eta}_{f}(\hat{\boldsymbol{v}})\\
+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}\left(\frac{1}{2}|\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}|^{2}-\hat{p}\right)(\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}-\hat{\boldsymbol{v}})\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}^{\eta}+\beta\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}\Big(\hat{\mathcal{S}}^{\eta}_{\Gamma}\Big)^{-1}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}-\hat{\boldsymbol{u}})\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\eta}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}-\hat{\boldsymbol{v}})\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\eta}\\
-\rho_{b}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\cdot\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}+2\mu_{e}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}\hat{\boldsymbol{D}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}):\hat{\boldsymbol{D}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}})+\lambda_{e}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}(\hat{\nabla}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}})(\hat{\nabla}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}})+2\mu_{v}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}\hat{\boldsymbol{D}}(\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}):\hat{\boldsymbol{D}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}})\\
+\lambda_{v}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}(\hat{\nabla}\cdot\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}})(\hat{\nabla}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}})-\alpha\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{\eta}_{b}\hat{p}\hat{\nabla}^{\eta}_{b}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}-c_{0}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}\hat{p}\cdot\partial_{t}\hat{r}-\alpha\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{\eta}_{b}\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\cdot\hat{\nabla}^{\eta}_{b}\hat{r}-\alpha\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}^{\eta})\hat{r}\\
+\kappa\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{\eta}_{b}\hat{\nabla}^{\eta}_{b}\hat{p}\cdot\hat{\nabla}^{\eta}_{b}\hat{r}-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}((\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}})\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}^{\eta})\hat{r}=\int_{\Omega_{f}(0)}\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}(0)+\rho_{b}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{0}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}(0)+c_{0}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}\hat{p}_{0}\cdot\hat{r}(0).

This weak formulation follows similarly as in the moving domain weak formulation derivation in Section
3.1
, with the exception of the integral
\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{f}(t)}\Big(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}+(\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{u}\Big)\cdot\boldsymbol{v}
being handled using (
29
). This can be handled for general vector-valued displacements using harmonic extension ALE maps as in
[
61
]
, and we include the calculation here for completeness. We have the following computation using (
27
) and (
29
), where
\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}|_{\Omega_{f}(t)}(x,y)=\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}\Big((\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\eta}_{f})^{-1}(x,y)\Big)
and
\boldsymbol{w}^{\eta}(x,y)=\hat{\boldsymbol{w}}\Big((\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\eta}_{f})^{-1}(x,y)\Big)
are defined on
\Omega_{f}(t)
, and where we integrate by parts and use the divergence free condition on
\boldsymbol{u}
:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{f}(t)}

\displaystyle\Big(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}+(\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{u}\Big)\cdot\boldsymbol{v}=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{f}(t)}\Big(\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}|_{\Omega_{f}(t)}+(\boldsymbol{u}-\boldsymbol{w}^{\eta})\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\Big)\cdot\boldsymbol{v}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{f}}\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{\eta}_{f}\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{f}(t)}\Big[((\boldsymbol{u}-\boldsymbol{w}^{\eta})\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u})\cdot\boldsymbol{v}-((\boldsymbol{u}-\boldsymbol{w}^{\eta})\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{v})\cdot\boldsymbol{u}\Big]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{f}(t)}(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{w}^{\eta})\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma(t)}(\boldsymbol{u}-\boldsymbol{w}^{\eta})\cdot\boldsymbol{n}(\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}).

By using the definition of the ALE velocity in (
25
) and the boundary conditions for the ALE map in (
24
), we deduce that
\hat{\boldsymbol{w}}^{\eta}|_{\Gamma(t)}=\boldsymbol{\xi}|_{\Gamma(t)}
. We also integrate by parts in time, change variables to the reference fluid domain
\hat{\Omega}_{f}
, use (
27
) and (
40
), and use the identity:

\partial_{t}\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{\eta}_{f}=\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{\eta}_{f}(\nabla^{\omega}_{f}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{w}}^{\eta})

to deduce that

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{f}(t)}\Big(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}+(\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{u}\Big)\cdot\boldsymbol{v}=-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{f}}\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{\eta}_{f}\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{v}}-\int_{\Omega_{f}(0)}\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}(0)\\
+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{f}}\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{\eta}_{f}\Big[((\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}-\hat{\boldsymbol{w}}^{\eta})\cdot\hat{\nabla}^{\eta}_{f}\hat{\boldsymbol{u}})\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{v}}-((\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}-\hat{\boldsymbol{w}}^{\eta})\cdot\hat{\nabla}^{\eta}_{f}\hat{\boldsymbol{v}})\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}\Big]-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{f}}(\partial_{t}\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{\eta}_{f})\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{v}}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}})\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}^{\eta}(\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{v}}).

This gives the weak formulation in Definition
3.2
in the fixed domain formulation.

We note that there are various terms in the weak formulation in Definition
3.2
for which weak solutions at the level of finite energy spaces do not suffice.

(1)
First
, we note that the Biot displacement
\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
, which in analogy to traditional bulk elasticity would have finite energy regularity of
H^{1}(\hat{\Omega}_{b})
, does not have enough regularity for a term like

-\alpha\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{\eta}_{b}\hat{p}\hat{\nabla}^{\eta}_{b}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi},

to make sense. This is because
\hat{p}\in H^{1}(\hat{\Omega}_{b})
in the finite energy space and
\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{\eta}_{b}:=\text{det}(\boldsymbol{I}+\nabla\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}})
in two spatial dimensions is only in
L^{1}(\hat{\Omega}_{b})
. In particular, the low regularity of geometric terms arising from the Biot displacement, such as the Jacobian
\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{\eta}_{b}
and the nonlinear operator
\nabla^{\eta}_{b}
, prevent us from properly interpreting the weak formulation in Definition
3.2
at the level of finite energy weak solutions.

(2)
Second
, the moving domains and moving interface are not well-defined. Since
\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\in H^{1}(\hat{\Omega}_{b})
,
\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
is not even a continuous function on
\hat{\Omega}_{b}
and its trace along the reference configuration
\hat{\Gamma}
of the interface is only in
H^{1/2}(\hat{\Gamma})
, which is also not continuous. Therefore, it is unclear if the Lagrangian map
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\eta}_{b}:\hat{\Omega}_{b}\to\Omega_{b}(t)
defined in (
3
) is injective or even well-defined. Similarly, the interface displacement
\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}|_{\hat{\Gamma}}
is not even a continuous function along
\hat{\Gamma}
, so the moving interface
\Gamma(t)
is not well-defined, and hence the fluid domain
\Omega_{f}(t)
in (
2
) is not well-defined
– this is in addition to geometric quantities involving the ALE map which depend on
\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}|_{\hat{\Gamma}}
.

Therefore, in order to analyze this FPSI problem, at least with the current tools we have, we need to have additional regularity. Both of the problems above arise from the low regularity of the Biot displacement
\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
, which prevents us from analyzing terms in the FPSI system arising from the moving Biot domain
\Omega_{b}(t)
and which prevents us from having a well-defined interface
\Gamma(t)
. While in past work, such as in
[
47
,
48
]
, the interface can be defined by having a reticular plate separating the Biot and fluid material (so that the interface displacement becomes the plate displacement which is in
H^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})
), in this problem with direct contact between the Biot material and the fluid, we must obtain the interface displacement directly from the Biot displacement. This motivates the use of a regularization method to analyze this FPSI problem with direct contact, which we refer to as the
regularized interface method
, which we describe in the next section.

4
The regularized interface method

4.1
Overview of method and the regularized Biot displacement

As mentioned in the previous section, the main challenges with regularity arise from the low regularity of the Biot displacement
\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
, which prevent us from properly interpreting the weak formulation of equations posed on the moving Biot domain and which prevents us from having a well-defined moving interface.
The main idea of the
regularized interface method
is to spatially regularize the Biot displacement
\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
to obtain a smooth regularized Biot displacement
\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\delta}
for a fixed but arbitrary regularization parameter
\delta>0
, which we can then use to define all geometric quantities in the problem and in the weak formulation.
Assuming the initial data is sufficiently well-behaved, this guarantees existence of a weak solution locally in time, for a time of existence potentially depending on the regularization parameter
\delta>0
.

Given a Biot displacement
\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}:\hat{\Omega}_{b}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
and a regularization parameter
\delta>0
, we define a regularized Biot displacement
\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
by convolving spatially with a convolution kernel with support of size
\delta
. Since we are working on a bounded domain
\hat{\Omega}_{b}
, we must use an extension to first extend
\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
to
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
before convolving. To do this, we recall the definition of a strong
1
-extension from Section 5.17 in
[
1
]
– such an extension exists for our current geometry in
\hat{\Omega}_{b}
, as discussed in Chapter 5 of
[
1
]
.

Definition 4.1

(Strong
1
-extension from
\hat{\Omega}_{b}
to
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
)
.

For the domain
\hat{\Omega}_{b}
, a
strong
1
-extension
is a linear operator
E
that acts on functions in
W^{k,p}(\hat{\Omega}_{b})
, for all
1\leq p<\infty
and
k=0,1
, such that for each
1\leq p<\infty
and
k=0,1
,
E:W^{k,p}(\hat{\Omega}_{b})\to W^{k,p}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
is a linear map such that
Ef|_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}=f
for all
f\in W^{k,p}(\hat{\Omega}_{b})
and there exists a uniform constant
C_{k,p}
(depending only on
k
and
p
) such that
\|Ef\|_{W^{k,p}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}\leq C\|f\|_{W^{k,p}(\hat{\Omega}_{b})}
.

We use this strong
1
-extension to define a regularized Biot displacement. Consider a compactly supported, nonnegative, radially symmetric, smooth function
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
with support being the closed ball of radius one in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
and define the standard convolution kernel
\varphi_{\delta}(z)=\delta^{-2}\varphi(\delta^{-1}z)
for
z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.

Definition 4.2

(Regularized Biot displacement and regularized domains)
.

Given a Biot displacement
\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}:\hat{\Omega}_{b}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
and a regularity parameter
\delta>0
, we define the
regularized Biot displacement

\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\delta}:\hat{\Omega}_{b}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
by

\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\delta}:=(E\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}*\varphi_{\delta})|_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}\text{ on }\hat{\Omega}_{b},

where
E
is a simple
H^{1}(\hat{\Omega}_{b})
extension, see Definition
4.1
. Then, we define the
regularized Lagrangian map
by

\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\eta^{\delta}}_{b}(t,\cdot):\hat{\Omega}_{b}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2},\qquad\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\eta^{\delta}}_{b}(t,\hat{x},\hat{y}):=(\hat{x},\hat{y})+\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\delta}(t,\hat{x},\hat{y})\quad\text{ for }(\hat{x},\hat{y})\in\hat{\Omega}_{b}.

We define the
regularized Biot domain

\Omega^{\delta}_{b}(t)
by

\Omega^{\delta}_{b}(t):=\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\eta^{\delta}}_{b}(t,\hat{\Omega}_{b}).

The trace
\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\delta}|_{\hat{\Gamma}}
of the regularized Biot displacement defines the
regularized interface displacement
, and hence, we define the
moving interface

\Gamma(t)
by

\Gamma(t):=\{(\hat{x},\hat{y})+\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\delta}|_{\hat{\Gamma}}(t,\hat{x},\hat{y}):(\hat{x},\hat{y})\in\hat{\Gamma}\},

and we define the
moving fluid domain

\Omega_{f}(t)
by

\Omega_{f}(t)=\hat{\Omega}\setminus(\Omega_{b}(t)\cup\Gamma(t)),

where we recall that
\hat{\Omega}:=\{\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}:|\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}|<2\}
is the total domain.

The definition above motivates the characterization of this regularization scheme as the
regularized interface method
. Namely, by regularizing the Biot displacement, we obtain a spatially smooth regularized Biot displacement
\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\delta}
, which is locally in time injective given that the initial data is sufficiently well-behaved. Then, we can use this smooth regularized Biot displacement to define all geometric quantities in the problem, which can be defined in a well-defined manner as long as the regularized Lagrangian map is bijective, since now the Lagrangian map is a smooth map. The idea will then be to use all of these regularized geometric quantities in the (regularized) weak formulation, instead of the previous geometric quantities defined using the finite energy Biot displacement
\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
, which does not possess sufficiently regularity to produce sufficiently spatially regular geometric terms in the weak formulation. However, the Biot displacement
\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
(before regularization) is what we will keep track of in the weak formulation and in most terms; we only use the regularized Biot displacement
\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\delta}
when dealing with any geometric quantities. We will hence now define a
regularized interface weak solution
by stating a new
regularized interface weak formulation
.

4.2
Definition of a regularized interface weak solution

We first define the solution and test spaces. We have the following solution spaces for the
fluid velocity on the moving domain
:

\begin{cases}V_{f}(t):=\{\boldsymbol{u}\in H^{1}(\Omega_{f}(t)):\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}=0\text{ on }\Omega_{f}(t)\text{ and }\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{0}\text{ on }\partial\Omega_{f}(t)\setminus\Gamma(t)\},\\
\mathcal{V}_{f}:=L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega_{f}(t)))\cap L^{2}(0,T;V_{f}(t)).\end{cases}

We can transfer this to the fixed domain by noting the transformation law for the divergence in (
28
), and we obtain the solution spaces for the
fluid velocity on the fixed domain
, given the interface displacement
\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\delta}|_{\hat{\Gamma}}
:

\begin{cases}V^{\eta^{\delta}}_{f}(t):=\{\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}\in H^{1}(\hat{\Omega}_{f}):\nabla^{\eta^{\delta}(t)}_{f}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}=0\text{ on }\hat{\Omega}_{f}\text{ and }\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}=\boldsymbol{0}\text{ on }\partial\hat{\Omega}_{f}\setminus\hat{\Gamma}\},\\
\mathcal{V}^{\eta^{\delta}}_{f}:=L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\hat{\Omega}_{f}))\cap L^{2}(0,T;V^{\eta^{\delta}}_{f}(t))\}.\end{cases}

(41)

Next, we define the following solution space for the
Biot displacement

\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
:

\begin{cases}V_{d}:=H^{1}(\hat{\Omega}_{b}),\\
