\mathcal{V}_{d}:=W^{1,\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\hat{\Omega}_{b}))\cap L^{\infty}(0,T;V_{d}).\end{cases}

(42)

and we define the following solution space for the
Biot pore pressure

\hat{p}
:

\begin{cases}V_{p}:=H^{1}(\hat{\Omega}_{b}),\\
\mathcal{V}_{p}:=L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\hat{\Omega}_{b}))\cap L^{2}(0,T;V_{p}).\end{cases}

(43)

Finally, we define the fully coupled solution spaces in the moving domain and fixed domain formulations respectively:

\begin{cases}\mathcal{V}:=\mathcal{V}_{f}\times\mathcal{V}_{d}\times\mathcal{V}_{p},\\
\mathcal{V}^{\eta^{\delta}}:=\mathcal{V}_{f}^{\eta^{\delta}}\times\mathcal{V}_{d}\times\mathcal{V}_{p},\end{cases}

(44)

and the fully coupled test spaces in the moving domain and fixed domain formulations respectively:

\begin{cases}\mathcal{Q}:=\{(\boldsymbol{v},\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}},\hat{r})\in C_{c}^{1}([0,T);V_{f}(t)\times V_{d}\times V_{p})\},\\
\mathcal{Q}^{\eta^{\delta}}:=\{(\hat{\boldsymbol{v}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}},\hat{r})\in C_{c}^{1}([0,T);V^{\eta^{\delta}}_{f}(t)\times V_{d}\times V_{p})\}.\end{cases}

(45)

Remark 4.1
.

We note that in the case of a Biot
poroviscoelastic
material with
\lambda_{v}>0
and
\mu_{v}>0
, see (
7
), the solution space
\mathcal{V}_{d}
for the Biot displacement
\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
is more specifically:

\mathcal{V}_{d}:=W^{1,\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\hat{\Omega}_{b}))\cap L^{\infty}(0,T;V_{d})\cap H^{1}(0,T;V_{d}).

However, since the case of a purely poroelastic Biot medium is more challenging (due to less regularization), we have defined the solution space for the Biot displacement above for this case, and
we adopt the convention throughout the paper of writing the existence proof for the more challenging case of a purely poroelastic Biot medium.

With the solution and test spaces defined, we can state the following regularized interface weak formulation for the FPSI problem with direct contact and nonlinear geometric coupling.

Definition 4.3

(Regularized interface weak solution: fixed domain formulation)
.

An ordered triple
(\hat{\boldsymbol{u}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}},\hat{p})\in\mathcal{V}^{\eta^{\delta}}
is a
regularized interface weak solution with regularization parameter
\delta>0
to the FPSI problem if the following
regularized interface weak formulation
holds for all test functions
(\hat{\boldsymbol{v}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}},\hat{r})\in\mathcal{Q}^{\eta^{\delta}}
, where the regularized Biot displacement, moving domains, and interface are defined in Definition
4.2
:

-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{f}}\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{\eta^{\delta}}_{f}\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{v}}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{f}}\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{\eta^{\delta}}_{f}\Big[((\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}-\hat{\boldsymbol{w}}^{\eta})\cdot\hat{\nabla}^{\eta^{\delta}}_{f}\hat{\boldsymbol{u}})\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{v}}-((\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}-\hat{\boldsymbol{w}}^{\eta})\cdot\hat{\nabla}^{\eta^{\delta}}_{f}\hat{\boldsymbol{v}})\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}\Big]\\
-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{f}}(\partial_{t}\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{\eta^{\delta}}_{f})\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{v}}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{\delta})\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}^{\eta^{\delta}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{v}})+2\nu\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{f}}\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{\eta^{\delta}}_{f}\hat{\boldsymbol{D}}^{\eta^{\delta}}_{f}(\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}):\hat{\boldsymbol{D}}^{\eta^{\delta}}_{f}(\hat{\boldsymbol{v}})\\
+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}\left(\frac{1}{2}|\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}|^{2}-\hat{p}\right)(\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{\delta}-\hat{\boldsymbol{v}})\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}^{\omega}+\beta\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}\Big(\hat{\mathcal{S}}^{\eta^{\delta}}_{\Gamma}\Big)^{-1}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{\delta}-\hat{\boldsymbol{u}})\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\omega}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{\delta}-\hat{\boldsymbol{v}})\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\omega}\\
-\rho_{b}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\cdot\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}+2\mu_{e}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}\hat{\boldsymbol{D}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}):\hat{\boldsymbol{D}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}})+\lambda_{e}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}(\hat{\nabla}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}})(\hat{\nabla}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}})+2\mu_{v}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}\hat{\boldsymbol{D}}(\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}):\hat{\boldsymbol{D}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}})\\
+\lambda_{v}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}(\hat{\nabla}\cdot\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}})(\hat{\nabla}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}})-\alpha\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{\eta^{\delta}}_{b}\hat{p}\hat{\nabla}^{\eta^{\delta}}_{b}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{\delta}-c_{0}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}\hat{p}\cdot\partial_{t}\hat{r}-\alpha\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{\eta^{\delta}}_{b}\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\delta}\cdot\hat{\nabla}^{\eta^{\delta}}_{b}\hat{r}-\alpha\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{\delta}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}^{\eta^{\delta}})\hat{r}\\
+\kappa\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{\eta^{\delta}}_{b}\hat{\nabla}^{\eta^{\delta}}_{b}\hat{p}\cdot\hat{\nabla}^{\eta^{\delta}}_{b}\hat{r}-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}((\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{\delta})\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}^{\eta^{\delta}})\hat{r}=\int_{\Omega_{f}(0)}\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}(0)+\rho_{b}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{0}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}(0)+c_{0}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}\hat{p}_{0}\cdot\hat{r}(0),

where
\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{\delta}
for the test function
\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}
is defined analogously via simple
H^{1}(\hat{\Omega}_{b})
extension and convolution as in Definition
4.2
, and the geometric quantities and operators are defined in Section
3.2
.

4.3
Discussion of regularized interface weak solution and a priori estimate

If one compares the weak formulation for the original FPSI problem (Definition
3.2
) and the regularized interface weak formulation for the regularized interface FPSI problem (Definition
4.3
), we can make several observations. First, we see that the moving domain geometry is regularized via the regularized Biot displacement
\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\delta}
and that any equations posed on the moving geometry, namely the pore pressure equation in the Biot equations (
4
), involve integrals over the
regularized moving Biot domain
. For example, the terms

-\alpha\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{\eta^{\delta}}_{b}\hat{p}\hat{\nabla}^{\eta^{\delta}}_{b}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{\delta}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad-\alpha\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{\eta^{\delta}}_{b}\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\delta}\cdot\hat{\nabla}^{\eta^{\delta}}_{b}\hat{r}

can be rewritten by a change of variables via the Lagrangian map as integrals over the
regularized Biot domain

\Omega^{\delta}_{b}(t)
, defined in Definition
4.2
. These integrals are now well-defined, since
\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\delta}
is smooth, in contrast to their counterparts in the original weak formulation (Definition
3.2
)

-\alpha\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{\eta}_{b}\hat{p}\hat{\nabla}^{\eta}_{b}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad-\alpha\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{\eta}_{b}\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\cdot\hat{\nabla}^{\eta}_{b}\hat{r},

which do not have enough spatial regularity (due to the limited
H^{1}(\hat{\Omega}_{b})
regularity of
\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
leading to low regularity for the geometric terms) to be properly interpreted.

When comparing the weak formulations for the original and the regularized interface FPSI problem, we see another set of differences arising from the
regularized interface condition
. While initially, for the direct problem, we obtained the movement of the fluid-Biot interface along which there is direct contact via a trace
\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}|_{\hat{\Gamma}}
along
\hat{\Gamma}
, in order to have sufficient regularity for the interface, we define the interface displacement in the regularized interface method via the trace of the
regularized Biot displacement

\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\delta}|_{\hat{\Gamma}}
. Therefore, some additional regularizations must be done in the weak formulation in order to take into account this regularized interface condition, in order to preserve the a priori energy estimate. For example, if we examine the following terms in the weak formulation

-\alpha\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{\eta^{\delta}}_{b}\hat{p}\hat{\nabla}^{\eta^{\delta}}_{b}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{\delta}-\alpha\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{\eta^{\delta}}_{b}\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\delta}\cdot\hat{\nabla}^{\eta^{\delta}}_{b}\hat{r}-\alpha\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{\delta}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}^{\eta^{\delta}})\hat{r},

we see the reason why the first term in the immediately preceding expression has a regularized test function
\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{\delta}
instead of just
\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}
and the second term has
\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\delta}
instead of
\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
. Namely, if we derive an energy inequality by substituting
\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
for the Biot displacement test function and
\hat{r}=\hat{p}
for the Biot pore pressure test function, these terms are consistent and cancel out, since
\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\delta}:=\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{\delta}
, which preserves the desired a priori energy estimate:

\displaystyle-\alpha\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{\eta^{\delta}}_{b}\hat{p}\hat{\nabla}^{\eta^{\delta}}_{b}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\delta}-\alpha\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{\eta^{\delta}}_{b}\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\delta}\cdot\hat{\nabla}^{\eta^{\delta}}_{b}\hat{p}-\alpha\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{\delta}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}^{\eta^{\delta}})\hat{p}

\displaystyle=-\alpha\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{\delta}_{b}(t)}p(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\delta})-\alpha\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{\delta}_{b}(t)}\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\delta}\cdot\nabla p-\alpha\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma(t)}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\delta}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})p=0,

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\text{ where }\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\delta}:=\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\delta}\circ(\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\eta^{\delta}}_{b})^{-1}\text{ and }\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\delta}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{\delta}\circ(\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\eta^{\delta}}_{b})^{-1}.

More generally, if we substitute
(\hat{\boldsymbol{v}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}},\hat{r})=(\hat{\boldsymbol{u}},\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}},\hat{p})
for the test functions into the regularized interface weak formulation (Definition
3.2
), we obtain the following energy estimate:

E_{f}(t)+E_{b}(t)+D(t)=E_{f}(0)+E_{b}(0),

(46)

where

\displaystyle E_{f}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega_{f}(t)}|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{f}}\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{\eta^{\delta}}_{f}|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2},\quad(\text{fluid kinetic energy}),

\displaystyle E_{b}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}|\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}|^{2}+\mu_{e}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}|\hat{\boldsymbol{D}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}})|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{e}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}|\hat{\nabla}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}c_{0}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}|\hat{p}|^{2},\quad(\text{Biot kinetic energy}),

\displaystyle D(t)

\displaystyle=2\nu\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{f}}\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{\eta^{\delta}}_{f}|\hat{\boldsymbol{D}}^{\eta^{\delta}}_{f}(\hat{\boldsymbol{u}})|^{2}+\beta\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}\Big(\hat{S}^{\eta^{\delta}}_{\Gamma}\Big)^{-1}|(\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{\delta}-\hat{\boldsymbol{u}})\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}^{\eta^{\delta}}|^{2}+2\mu_{v}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}|\hat{\boldsymbol{D}}(\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}})|^{2}

\displaystyle\ \ \ +\lambda_{v}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}|\hat{\nabla}\cdot\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}|^{2}+\kappa\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{\eta^{\delta}}_{b}|\hat{\nabla}^{\eta^{\delta}}_{b}\hat{p}|^{2},\quad(\text{dissipation}).

In this sense, the regularization of the weak formulation found in the regularized interface weak formulation (Definition
4.3
) is the minimal regularization of the original weak formulation (Definition
3.2
) such that:

1.

All terms in the weak formulation are well-defined at the level of finite energy solutions.

2.

The regularization does not alter the a priori energy estimate.

4.4
Main results and proof strategy

The main result of this manuscript is the existence of a regularized interface weak solution to the FPSI problem with direct contact and nonlinear geometric coupling, for each fixed but arbitrary regularization parameter
\delta>0
.

Theorem 4.1
.

Consider a fixed regularization parameter
\delta>0
and either a purely poroelastic or poroviscoelastic Biot medium, see (
7
). Suppose that we are given a divergence-free initial fluid velocity
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in L^{2}(\Omega_{f}(0))
, an initial Biot displacement
\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{0}\in H^{1}(\hat{\Omega}_{b})
, an initial Biot velocity
\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{0}\in L^{2}(\hat{\Omega}_{b})
, and an initial pore pressure
\hat{p}_{0}\in L^{2}(\hat{\Omega}_{b})
. Assume that the following geometric nondegeneracy conditions on the initial data hold:

•

The initial Lagrangian map
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\eta^{\delta}_{0}}_{b}:\hat{\Omega}_{b}\to\Omega^{\delta}_{b}(0)
is bijective and satisfies
\text{det}(\boldsymbol{I}+\hat{\nabla}\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{0}^{\delta})\geq c>0
and
|\boldsymbol{I}+\hat{\nabla}\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\delta}_{0}|\geq c>0
on
\overline{\Omega_{b}}
for some positive constant
c>0
.

•

The initial fluid ALE map
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\eta^{\delta}_{0}}_{f}:\hat{\Omega}_{f}\to\Omega_{f}(0)
is bijective and satisfies
\text{det}(\hat{\nabla}\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\eta^{\delta}_{0}}_{f})\geq c>0
and
|\hat{\nabla}\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\eta^{\delta}_{0}}_{f}|\geq c>0
on
\overline{\Omega_{f}}
for some positive constant
c>0
.

•

The parametrization of the initial moving regularized direct contact interface:

\boldsymbol{r}(\hat{z})=\Big(\cos(\hat{z})+\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\delta}_{0}|_{\hat{\Gamma}}(\hat{z}),\sin(\hat{z})+\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\delta}_{0}|_{\hat{\Gamma}}(\hat{z})\Big),\quad\hat{z}\in[0,2\pi],

has
\|\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}(\hat{z})\|\geq\alpha>0
for all
\hat{z}\in[0,2\pi]
for some positive constant
\alpha>0
.

In addition, assume that the initial fluid velocity satisfies the compatibility condition (
16
). Then, for some time
T>0
(depending potentially on
\delta>0
), there exists a regularized interface weak solution
(\hat{\boldsymbol{u}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}},\hat{p})
to the direct contact FPSI problem with nonlinear geometric coupling in the sense of Definition
4.3
on the time interval
[0,T]
.

This existence result is novel since there is direct contact between the Biot medium and the fluid. The only past existence result on Biot-fluid FPSI with nonlinear geometric coupling is in
[
47
,
48
]
, with a plate at the Biot-fluid interface, whose elastodynamics regularizes the interface, for which there is existence to a slightly different regularized weak formulation (without a regularized interface condition).

Therefore, the proof strategy for establishing Theorem
4.1
will be to use an approximation parameter
h>0
, where the approximate problem will be the problem with a plate of thickness
h>0
at the fluid-structure interface
\hat{\Gamma}
. Namely, the approximate problem will have a plate of thickness
h
, and the interface displacement
\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}
will be the (vector-valued) displacement of the plate from its reference configuration
\hat{\Gamma}
. To impose the condition in the regularized interface method that the interface displacement is determined by the trace
\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\delta}|_{\hat{\Gamma}}
, we require the following regularized interface kinematic coupling condition:

\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\delta}|_{\hat{\Gamma}}.

Furthermore, the plate of thickness
h>0
at the interface will have inertia and will hence have its own elastodynamics that affects the entire coupled system. Specifically, the interface displacement (which is now the plate displacement) will be modeled by a viscoelastic plate equation given by:

h\partial_{t}^{2}\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}+h\hat{\Delta}^{2}\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}+h\hat{\Delta}^{2}\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}=\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{p}\text{ on }\hat{\Gamma},\qquad\text{ for }\hat{\Delta}:=\partial^{2}_{\hat{z}},

(47)

where we consider this equation for
\hat{z}\in[0,2\pi]
with periodic boundary conditions using the natural parametrization of
\hat{\Gamma}
in (
33
). In addition, instead of direct balance of normal stress (in the case of direct contact in (
13
)), we have a
dynamic coupling condition
in the approximate problem with the plate, specifying the load on the elastodynamics of the plate
\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{p}
as the difference in normal stress from the Biot medium and fluid:

\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{p}=-\text{det}(\nabla\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\omega}_{f})[\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{f}(\nabla\boldsymbol{u},\pi)\circ\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\omega}_{f}](\nabla\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\omega}_{f})^{-t}\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}+\hat{S}_{b}(\hat{\nabla}\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}},\hat{p})\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}|_{\hat{\Gamma}},

(48)

where the ALE map
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\omega}_{f}
is defined via (
30
) and (
31
), using the plate displacement
\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}
.

We then show existence of a regularized interface weak solution (satisfying a suitably regularized weak formulation) to the approximate problem with a plate of thickness
h>0
at the interface for each parameter
h>0
. This will be done via a constructive existence scheme using Lie operator splitting, in which we discretize in time into
N
total subintervals of length
\Delta t
. Upon obtaining approximate solutions for each
N
via Lie operator splitting, we then obtain uniform bounds in
N
and use compactness arguments (Aubin-Lions) to obtain strongly convergent subsequences and pass to the limit as
N\to\infty
in the semidiscrete weak formulations to obtain a regularized weak solution to the problem with a plate of thickness
h>0
. At this stage, we have a regularized interface weak solution for each
h>0
, and from our previous analysis of constructive existence, we can show that
the interval of existence is independent of the parameter
h
, so that all regularized weak interface solutions can be constructed on a common interval
[0,T]
independently of
h
, but which potentially depends on
\delta>0
which is fixed.
We then take the singular limit in the resulting regularized interface weak solutions (to the regularized problem with plate with thickness parameter
h>0
) as
h\to 0
to obtain a regularized weak interface solution which satisfies the limiting weak formulation with direct contact in Definition
4.3
. This will involve obtaining uniform bounds independently of
h
, and then passing to the limit using compactness arguments. This gives us a limiting regularized interface solution as
h\to 0
satisfying the regularized interface weak formulation (with
h=0
) in Definition
4.3
, which will complete the proof.

Before we show existence of a regularized interface weak solution to the approximate problem with a plate of thickness
h>0
in the sense of Definition
5.1
, we make some comments about the choice of the plate equation (
47
) approximate problem.

1.

Presence of the plate.
Having a plate at the boundary is advantageous as it simplifies constructive existence of solutions to the nonlinearly coupled FPSI problem. The main advantage is that in the constructive existence proof which uses a splitting scheme, the plate displacement (which updates the moving interface configuration and hence determines the geometry of the problem) can be updated independently (in a separate subproblem) from the Biot/fluid quantities, which are posed on domains that rely on the interface location. This enables us to separate the time-discrete moving interface update and the dynamical update of the Biot/fluid dynamical quantities, which allows us to construct a stable scheme. In particular, we can pose the Biot/fluid subproblem on a fixed geometry that is determined already by a prior plate subproblem update in the prior step, which at the approximate level allows us to solve the Biot/fluid subproblem on a fixed domain at each time-discrete step.

2.

Second order viscoelasticity and regularization of plate.
Note in addition that in the equation (
47
), there is second order viscoelasticity and regularization, so both the plate displacement
\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
and the plate velocity
\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
are in
H^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})
. The plate displacement
\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
being in
H^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})
is needed since we have a
vector-valued
(rather than purely transverse)
plate displacement
, and the stability results we use for preventing geometric degeneracies rely on having
C^{1}
interfaces, see Proposition
3.1
. We also need
H^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})
estimates on the plate velocity
\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
, because we need to propagate uniform geometric estimates on
\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{\omega}_{f}
and
\nabla\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\omega}_{f}
defined in (
32
) involving
\nabla\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
in time. This requires uniform pointwise estimates on the time derivative of
\nabla\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
, for which we need the plate velocity to be in
H^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})
since the plate is one-dimensional, see Proposition
5.2
.
We emphasize that the second-order viscoelasticity is needed only for the construction via splitting scheme, which staggers the interface (geometric) update and the fluid/Biot update, and this requirement vanishes in the limit as
h\to 0
once we have a limiting solution that is no longer time-discrete.

5
The approximate problem with reticular plate of thickness
h>0

We consider the approximate problem with a plate of thickness
h>0
at the interface, described by the elastodynamics equation (
47
) and the dynamic coupling condition specifying the load on the plate in (
48
). In this case, the movement of the interface is determined by the (vector-valued) displacement
\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}
of the reticular plate from its reference configuration
\hat{\Gamma}
.

We define a concept of a regularized interface weak solution for the problem with plate thickness (approximation) parameter
h>0
. Throughout the analysis, we will consider the regularization parameter
\delta>0
to be a fixed but arbitrary parameter (even in the singular limit as
h\to 0
which will be used to obtain a regularized interface weak solution for the problem with direct contact, we will keep
\delta>0
fixed). Since the plate equation has finite energy solutions in
H^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})
, we have that the solution space and test space for the approximate problem are as follows, in analogy to the direct contact solution/test spaces in (
44
) and (
45
). In contrast to the direct contact solution space (which does not include the interface displacement
\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}
since this is just the trace of the Biot displacement), the solution space for the problem with parameter
h>0
has the plate displacement included. We define the following (fixed domain) solution space and test space:

\begin{cases}\mathcal{V}_{h}^{\omega}:=H^{1}(0,T;H^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})),\\
\mathcal{V}_{h}^{\omega}:=\{(\hat{\boldsymbol{u}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}},\hat{p})\in\mathcal{V}_{f}^{\omega}\times\mathcal{V}_{h}^{\omega}\times\mathcal{V}_{d}\times\mathcal{V}_{p},\;\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}:=\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\delta}|_{\hat{\Gamma}}\},\\
\mathcal{Q}_{h}^{\omega}:=\{(\hat{\boldsymbol{v}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}},\hat{r})\in C_{c}^{1}([0,T);V^{\omega}_{f}(t)\times H^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})\times V_{d}\times V_{p}):\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}:=\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{\delta}|_{\hat{\Gamma}}\},\end{cases}

where
\mathcal{V}_{d}
and
\mathcal{V}_{p}
are defined in (
42
) and (
43
), and the fluid space is now indexed by the plate displacement
\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}
. In analogy to (
41
), we also define the moving domain solution spaces:

\begin{cases}V^{\omega}_{f}:=\{\boldsymbol{u}\in H^{1}(\hat{\Omega}_{f}):\nabla^{\omega}_{f}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}=0\text{ on }\hat{\Omega}_{f}\text{ and }\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}=\boldsymbol{0}\text{ on }\partial\hat{\Omega}_{f}\setminus\hat{\Gamma}\},\\
\mathcal{V}^{\omega}_{f}:=L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\hat{\Omega}_{f}))\cap L^{2}(0,T;V^{\omega}_{f}(t)),\end{cases}

(49)

where we recall the definition of the nonlinear differential operators indexed by
\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}
in (
32
). We also define the following rescaled normal and tangent vectors in analogy to (
40
) given the vector-valued plate displacement
\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}:=(\hat{\omega}_{1},\hat{\omega}_{2})
:

\boldsymbol{n}^{\omega}:=(\cos(\hat{z})+\partial_{\hat{z}}\hat{\omega}_{2},\sin(\hat{z})-\partial_{\hat{z}}\hat{\omega}_{1}),\qquad\boldsymbol{\tau}^{\omega}:=(-\sin(\hat{z})+\partial_{\hat{z}}\hat{\omega}_{1},\cos(\hat{z})+\partial_{\hat{z}}\hat{\omega}_{2}).

(50)

We then have the following
regularized interface weak formulation
for the regularized interface weak solutions to the problem with plate of thickness
h>0
.

Definition 5.1

(Regularized interface weak solution: problem with plate of thickness
h
)
.

An ordered collection
(\hat{\boldsymbol{u}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}},\hat{p})\in\mathcal{V}^{\omega}_{h}
is a regularized interface weak solution to the problem with regularization parameter
\delta>0
and a plate of thickness
h>0
if
\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}:=\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\delta}|_{\hat{\Gamma}}
and the following
regularized interface weak formulation
holds for all test functions
(\hat{\boldsymbol{v}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}},\hat{r})\in\mathcal{Q}^{\omega}_{h}
:

-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{f}}\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{\omega}_{f}\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{v}}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{f}}\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{\omega}_{f}\Big[((\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}-\hat{\boldsymbol{w}}^{\eta})\cdot\hat{\nabla}^{\omega}_{f}\hat{\boldsymbol{u}})\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{v}}-((\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}-\hat{\boldsymbol{w}}^{\eta})\cdot\hat{\nabla}^{\omega}_{f}\hat{\boldsymbol{v}})\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}\Big]\\
-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{f}}(\partial_{t}\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{\omega}_{f})\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{v}}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{\delta})\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}^{\omega}(\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{v}})+2\nu\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{f}}\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{\omega}_{f}\hat{\boldsymbol{D}}^{\omega}_{f}(\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}):\hat{\boldsymbol{D}}^{\omega}_{f}(\hat{\boldsymbol{v}})\\
+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}\left(\frac{1}{2}|\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}|^{2}-\hat{p}\right)(\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{\delta}-\hat{\boldsymbol{v}})\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}^{\omega}+\beta\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}\Big(\hat{\mathcal{S}}^{\omega}_{\Gamma}\Big)^{-1}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{\delta}-\hat{\boldsymbol{u}})\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\omega}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{\delta}-\hat{\boldsymbol{v}})\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\omega}\\
-h\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\cdot\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}+h\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}\Delta\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\cdot\Delta\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}+h\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}\hat{\Delta}\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\cdot\hat{\Delta}\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}-\rho_{b}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\cdot\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}+2\mu_{e}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}\hat{\boldsymbol{D}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}):\hat{\boldsymbol{D}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}})\\
+\lambda_{e}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}(\hat{\nabla}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}})(\hat{\nabla}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}})+2\mu_{v}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}\hat{\boldsymbol{D}}(\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}):\hat{\boldsymbol{D}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}})+\lambda_{v}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}(\hat{\nabla}\cdot\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}})(\hat{\nabla}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}})\\
-\alpha\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{\eta^{\delta}}_{b}\hat{p}\hat{\nabla}^{\eta^{\delta}}_{b}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{\delta}-c_{0}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}\hat{p}\cdot\partial_{t}\hat{r}-\alpha\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{\eta^{\delta}}_{b}\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\delta}\cdot\hat{\nabla}^{\eta^{\delta}}_{b}\hat{r}-\alpha\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}^{\omega})\hat{r}\\
+\kappa\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{\eta^{\delta}}_{b}\hat{\nabla}^{\eta^{\delta}}_{b}\hat{p}\cdot\hat{\nabla}^{\eta^{\delta}}_{b}\hat{r}-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}((\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\zeta}})\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}^{\omega})\hat{r}=\int_{\Omega_{f}(0)}\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}(0)+\rho_{b}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{0}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}(0)+c_{0}\int_{\hat{\Omega}_{b}}\hat{p}_{0}\cdot\hat{r}(0),

Remark 5.1

(Notational convention in the approximate problem with a plate)
.

In the approximate problem, we have a viscoelastic plate of thickness
h>0
at the interface that has its own vector-valued displacement
\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}
, which satisfies continuity of displacements at the interface,
\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}:=\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\delta}|_{\hat{\Gamma}}
. Therefore, to emphasize the presence of the plate, whose displacement directly specifies the time-dependent interface
\Gamma(t)
, we index all geometric quantities in the fluid and plate by
\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}
instead of
\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\delta}
, as in
\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{\omega}_{f}
,
\hat{\nabla}^{\omega}_{f}
,
\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}^{\omega}
, and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\omega}
, see the discussion around (
30
) and (
31
), and the definitions in (
50
). While for example by (
23
) and (
24
), we can equivalently denote the map
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\omega}_{f}
by
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\eta^{\delta}}_{f}
, we choose to use
\omega
in Definition
5.1
to emphasize the presence of the plate, whereas in contrast, we use
\eta^{\delta}
to index geometric quantities in the regularized interface direct contact weak formulation in Definition
4.3
to emphasize the fact that there is direct contact (no plate).

The goal is then (1) to establish existence of regularized weak solutions for the regularized problem of the form above (with the additional regularized kinematic coupling condition) and then (2) derive uniform estimates, which are independent of the plate thickness parameter
h>0
, which will allow us to take the singular limit of solutions as
h\to 0
for fixed regularization parameter
\delta>0
to obtain existence of weak solutions for a limiting problem without a plate separating the poroelastic medium and the fluid.
Note that an important part of this analysis involves showing that the weak solutions to the regularized problem with regularization parameter
\delta>0
and plate thickness
h>0
exist on a uniform time interval of existence that is independent of
h>0
.
In particular, the goal of this section is to prove the following result:

Theorem 5.1
.

Consider a fixed regularization parameter
\delta>0
and consider an initial plate displacement and plate velocity
\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{0},\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{0}\in H^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})
and in addition: an initial fluid velocity
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
, initial Biot displacement
\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{0}
, an initial Biot velocity
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{0}
, and an initial pore pressure
\hat{p}_{0}
satisfying the conditions listed in Theorem
4.1
, along with the regularized kinematic coupling condition:
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0}=\boldsymbol{\eta}_{0}^{\delta}|_{\Gamma}
and
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{0}=\boldsymbol{\xi}_{0}^{\delta}|_{\Gamma}
. Then, for some time
T>0
(depending only on
\delta>0
) and
independent of the plate thickness
h>0
, there exists a regularized interface weak solution
(\hat{\boldsymbol{u}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}},\hat{p})
to the FPSI problem with nonlinear geometric coupling and a reticular plate of thickness
h>0
in the sense of Definition
5.1
.

Note that by formally substituting for the test function
(\hat{\boldsymbol{v}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}},\hat{r})=(\hat{\boldsymbol{u}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}},\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}},\hat{p})
, we obtain the following a priori energy estimate, which is the analogue of the energy estimate in (
46
), where the terms
E_{f}(t)
,
E_{b}(0)
, and
D(t)
are defined, but with the addition of energy arising from the plate (which depends on the plate thickness
h>0
):

\displaystyle E_{f}

\displaystyle(t)+E_{b}(t)+E_{h}(t)+D_{h}(t)=E_{f}(0)+E_{b}(0)+E_{h}(0),

\displaystyle\text{ where }E_{h}(t):=\frac{1}{2}h\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}|\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}h\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}|\hat{\Delta}\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}|^{2}\text{ and }D_{h}(t):=D(t)+h\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}|\hat{\Delta}\partial_{t}\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}|^{2}.

We note that
E_{h}(t)
is the contribution arising to the energy arising from the plate (scaled by the plate thickness
h>0
), and
D_{h}(t)
is the usual dissipation
D(t)
from (
46
) with the additional dissipation arising from the viscoelasticity of the plate.

A remark on a notational convention.
To simplify the notation in the remainder of the manuscript, from this point onwards, we will omit the “hat” notation when referring to reference domains and functions/differential operators defined on reference domains. We will instead rely on the context to disambiguate functions/differential operators defined on reference and moving (time-dependent) domains. For example, integrands can be disambiguated by observing whether the domain of integration is a reference domain (such as
\Omega_{f}
,
\Omega_{b}
, or
\Gamma
) or a moving domain (such as
\Omega_{f}(t)
,
\Omega_{b}(t)
, or
\Gamma(t)
).

5.1
A constructive splitting scheme

We use the following splitting scheme to semidiscretize the problem in time and construct approximate solutions. We split the entire time interval
[0,T]
into
N
subintervals
[t_{n},t_{n+1}]
where
t_{n}=n\Delta t
for
n=0,1,...,N-1
and solve the following two subproblems iteratively to construct approximate solutions. We will denote the (spatially non-discrete and time-discrete) approximate solution on
[t_{n},t_{n+1}]
by

\left(\boldsymbol{u}^{n+\frac{i}{2}}_{N},\boldsymbol{\omega}^{n+\frac{i}{2}}_{N},\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n+\frac{i}{2}}_{N},\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n+\frac{i}{2}}_{N},p^{n+\frac{i}{2}}_{N}\right),\qquad\text{ for }i=1,2

where
\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n+\frac{i}{2}}_{N}
is the approximate (vector-valued) plate velocity. The approximate vector for each
n
at
i=1
represents the result after running the plate subproblem and the approximate vector for each
n
at
i=2
represents the result after running the Biot/fluid subproblem. We use the initial data to initialize the splitting scheme:

\Big(\boldsymbol{u}^{0}_{N},\boldsymbol{\omega}^{N},\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{0}_{N},\boldsymbol{\eta}^{0}_{N},p^{0}_{N}\Big):=\Big(\boldsymbol{u}_{0},\boldsymbol{\eta}_{0}^{\delta}|_{\Gamma},\boldsymbol{\xi}_{0}^{\delta}|_{\Gamma},\boldsymbol{\eta}_{0},p_{0}\Big)

Note that in accordance with the notational convention just introduced, we have omitted the hat notation on these approximate semidiscrete solutions.

This constructive splitting scheme shows why the plate approximation is helpful for obtaining existence, as to obtain a stable scheme, it is useful to have a separate subproblem for the interface update, which is accomplished in this case by giving the interface mass/inertia. Thus, the plate (interface) displacement in the splitting scheme evolves dynamically via a
separate elastodynamics equation
for the movement of the interface.

Plate subproblem.
In the plate subproblem, we solve the viscoelastic plate equation with zero external forcing, in order to update the interface displacement (which will hence be used in the following Biot/fluid subproblem to define the semidiscrete moving domains). We keep
\boldsymbol{u}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}_{N}=\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{N}
,
\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}_{N}=\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n}_{N}
, and
p^{n+\frac{1}{2}}_{N}=p^{n}_{N}
unchanged. The
plate subproblem
is to find
\boldsymbol{\omega}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}_{N}\in H^{2}(\Gamma)
and
\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}_{N}\in H^{2}(\Gamma)
such that:

\int_{\Gamma}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{\omega}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}_{N}-\boldsymbol{\omega}^{n-\frac{1}{2}}_{N}}{\Delta t}\right)\cdot\phi=\int_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}_{N}\cdot\phi,\qquad\text{ for all }\phi\in L^{2}(\Gamma),

(51)

h\int_{\Gamma}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}_{N}-\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n}_{N}}{\Delta t}\right)\cdot\varphi+h\int_{\Gamma}\Delta\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}_{N}\cdot\Delta\boldsymbol{\varphi}+h\int_{\Gamma}\Delta\boldsymbol{\omega}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}_{N}\cdot\Delta\boldsymbol{\varphi}=0,\qquad\text{ for all }\varphi\in H^{2}(\Gamma),

(52)

where we are solving the plate elastodynamics equation in weak formulation with zero external loading. Existence and uniqueness for the plate subproblem is classically established using the Lax-Milgram lemma,
[
35
]
. By substituting
\boldsymbol{\varphi}=\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}_{N}
and using the facts that
\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}_{N}=\frac{\boldsymbol{\omega}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}_{N}-\boldsymbol{\omega}^{n-\frac{1}{2}}_{N}}{\Delta t}
and
\boldsymbol{\omega}^{n}_{N}=\boldsymbol{\omega}^{n-\frac{1}{2}}_{N}
, we obtain the following energy estimate:

\frac{1}{2}h\int_{\Gamma}|\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}_{N}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}h\int_{\Gamma}|\Delta\boldsymbol{\omega}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}_{N}|^{2}+(\Delta t)h\int_{\Gamma}|\Delta\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}_{N}|^{2}=\frac{1}{2}h\int_{\Gamma}|\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n}_{N}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}h\int_{\Gamma}|\Delta\boldsymbol{\omega}^{n}_{N}|^{2}.

(53)

Biot-fluid subproblem.
We keep the plate displacement unchanged
\omega^{n+1}_{N}=\omega^{n+\frac{1}{2}}_{N}
. We recall the function spaces
V^{\omega}_{f}
,
V_{d}
, and
V_{p}
from (
49
), (
42
), and (
43
). The
Biot-fluid subproblem
is to find
(\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1}_{N},\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n+1}_{N},\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n+1}_{N},p^{n+1}_{N})\in V^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}\times H^{2}(\Gamma)\times V_{d}\times V_{p}
such that the following weak formulation is satisfied for all test functions
(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{\varphi},\boldsymbol{\psi},r)\in V^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}\times H^{2}(\Gamma)\times V_{d}\times V_{p}
satisfying the regularized kinematic coupling condition
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\delta}|_{\Gamma}=\boldsymbol{\varphi}
:

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{f}}\mathcal{J}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}\dot{\boldsymbol{u}}^{n+1}_{N}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}+2\nu\int_{\Omega_{f}}\mathcal{J}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}\boldsymbol{D}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1}_{N}):\boldsymbol{D}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}(\boldsymbol{v})+\int_{\Gamma}\left(\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1}_{N}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{N}-p^{n+1}_{N}\right)(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\delta}-\boldsymbol{v})\cdot\boldsymbol{n}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}

(54)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega_{f}}\mathcal{J}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}\left[\left(\left(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{N}-\boldsymbol{w}^{n+1}_{N}\right)\cdot\nabla^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1}_{N}\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{v}-\left(\left(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{N}-\boldsymbol{w}^{n+1}_{N}\right)\cdot\nabla^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}\boldsymbol{v}\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1}_{N}\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega_{f}}\left(\frac{\mathcal{J}^{\omega^{n+1}_{N}}_{f}-\mathcal{J}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}}{\Delta t}\right)\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1}_{N}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}\left(\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1}_{N}-\left(\dot{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{n+1}_{N}\right)^{\delta}\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{n}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{N}\cdot\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle+\beta\int_{\Gamma}\Big(\mathcal{S}^{\omega}_{\Gamma}\Big)^{-1}\left(\left(\boldsymbol{\dot{\eta}}^{n+1}_{N}\right)^{\delta}-\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1}_{N}\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\delta}-\boldsymbol{v})\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}+\rho_{b}\int_{\Omega_{b}}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{\dot{\eta}}^{n+1}_{N}-\boldsymbol{\dot{\eta}}^{n}_{N}}{\Delta t}\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}

\displaystyle+h\int_{\Gamma}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n+1}_{N}-\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}_{N}}{\Delta t}\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{\varphi}+2\mu_{e}\int_{\Omega_{b}}\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n+1}_{N}):\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{\psi})+\lambda_{e}\int_{\Omega_{b}}(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n+1}_{N})(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi})

\displaystyle+2\mu_{v}\int_{\Omega_{b}}\boldsymbol{D}(\dot{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{n+1}_{N}):\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{\psi})+\lambda_{v}\int_{\Omega_{b}}(\nabla\cdot\dot{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{n+1}_{N})(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi})-\alpha\int_{\Omega_{b}}\mathcal{J}^{(\eta^{n}_{N})^{\delta}}_{b}p^{n+1}_{N}\nabla^{(\eta^{n}_{N})^{\delta}}_{b}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\delta}

\displaystyle+c_{0}\int_{\Omega_{b}}\frac{p^{n+1}_{N}-p^{n}_{N}}{\Delta t}r-\alpha\int_{\Omega_{b}}\mathcal{J}^{(\eta^{n}_{N})^{\delta}}_{b}\left(\boldsymbol{\dot{\eta}}^{n+1}_{N}\right)^{\delta}\cdot\nabla^{(\eta^{n}_{N})^{\delta}}_{b}r-\alpha\int_{\Gamma}\left(\left(\boldsymbol{\dot{\eta}}^{n+1}_{N}\right)^{\delta}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}\right)r

\displaystyle+\kappa\int_{\Omega_{b}}\mathcal{J}^{(\eta^{n}_{N})^{\delta}}_{b}\nabla^{(\eta^{n}_{N})^{\delta}}_{b}p^{n+1}_{N}\cdot\nabla^{(\eta^{n}_{N})^{\delta}}_{b}r-\int_{\Gamma}\left[\left(\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1}_{N}-\left(\boldsymbol{\dot{\eta}}^{n+1}_{N}\right)^{\delta}\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{n}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}\right]r=0,

and

\int_{\Gamma}\left(\frac{\left(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n+1}_{N}\right)^{\delta}-\left(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n}_{N}\right)^{\delta}}{\Delta t}\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{\phi}=\int_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n+1}_{N}\cdot\boldsymbol{\phi},\qquad\text{ for all }\boldsymbol{\phi}\in L^{2}(\Gamma),

(55)

where the discrete ALE velocity is defined by:

\boldsymbol{w}^{n+1}_{N}=\frac{1}{\Delta t}\Big(\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\omega^{n+1}_{N}}_{f}-\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}\Big),

(56)

and where we use the shorthand “dot” notation for discrete time derivatives:

\dot{f}^{n}_{N}=\frac{f^{n}_{N}-f^{n-1}_{N}}{\Delta t}.

We emphasize that to derive this weak formulation, we must use the fact that
\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n+1}_{N}|_{\Gamma}=(\boldsymbol{\dot{\eta}}^{n+1}_{N})^{\delta}
.

Existence and uniqueness for the Biot-fluid subproblem.
Due to the additional regularization in the kinematic coupling condition, the well-posedness for the Biot-fluid subproblem is different from that found in other works on nonlinearly coupled FPSI (such as in Section 6.2 of
[
48
]
), so we present the full argument and emphasize the parts that change due to the additional kinematic regularization.

The goal will be to eliminate the variable
\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n+1}_{N}
from the weak formulation so that we are solving for
(\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1}_{N},\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n+1}_{N},p^{n+1}_{N})
. By the regularized kinematic coupling condition which is deduced from (
55
):

\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n+1}_{N}=\left(\frac{\left(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n+1}_{N}\right)^{\delta}-\left(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n}_{N}\right)^{\delta}}{\Delta t}\right).

We will also use the rescaling of test functions
v\to(\Delta t)v
and
r\to(\Delta t)r
, and we hence obtain the following weak formulation of the problem: find
(\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1}_{N},\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n+1}_{N},p^{n+1}_{N})\in V^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}\times V_{d}\times V_{p}
such that for all test functions
(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{\psi},r)\in V^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}\times V_{d}\times V_{p}
:

B[\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1}_{N},\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n+1}_{N},\boldsymbol{\psi},p^{n+1}_{N},r]=(\Delta t)^{2}\int_{\Omega_{f}}\mathcal{J}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{N}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}-\frac{1}{2}(\Delta t)^{2}\int_{\Gamma}\left(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n}_{N}\right)^{\delta}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{N}\cdot\boldsymbol{v})\\
+\beta(\Delta t)\int_{\Gamma}\Big(\mathcal{S}^{\omega}_{\Gamma}\Big)^{-1}\left(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n}_{N}\right)^{\delta}\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\delta}-(\Delta t)\boldsymbol{v})\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}+\rho_{b}\int_{\Omega_{b}}(2\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n}_{N}-\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n-1}_{N})\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}\\
+h\int_{\Gamma}\left(\left(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n}_{N}\right)^{\delta}+(\Delta t)\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}_{N}\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\delta}+2\mu_{v}(\Delta t)\int_{\Omega_{b}}\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n}_{N}):\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{\psi})+\lambda_{v}(\Delta t)\int_{\Omega_{b}}(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n}_{N})(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi})\\
+c_{0}(\Delta t)^{2}\int_{\Omega_{b}}p^{n}_{N}r-\alpha(\Delta t)^{2}\int_{\Omega_{b}}\mathcal{J}^{(\eta^{n}_{N})^{\delta}}_{b}\left(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n}_{N}\right)^{\delta}\cdot\nabla^{(\eta^{n}_{N})^{\delta}}_{b}r-\alpha(\Delta t)^{2}\int_{\Gamma}\left(\left(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n}_{N}\right)^{\delta}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}\right)r+(\Delta t)^{2}\int_{\Gamma}\left(\left(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n}_{N}\right)^{\delta}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}\right)r,

(57)

for the bilinear form
B:V^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}\times V_{d}\times V_{p}\times V^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}\times V_{d}\times V_{p}
defined by:

\displaystyle B[\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{\eta},\boldsymbol{\psi},p,r]:=(\Delta t)^{2}\int_{\Omega_{f}}\mathcal{J}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}(\Delta t)^{3}\int_{\Omega_{f}}\mathcal{J}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}\Big[\Big((\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{N}-\boldsymbol{w}^{n+1}_{N})\cdot\nabla^{\omega^{n}_{N}}\boldsymbol{u}\Big)\cdot\boldsymbol{v}-\Big((\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{N}-\boldsymbol{w}^{n+1}_{N})\cdot\nabla^{\omega^{n}_{N}}\boldsymbol{v}\Big)\cdot\boldsymbol{u}\Big]

\displaystyle+\frac{(\Delta t)^{2}}{2}\int_{\Omega_{f}}\Big(\mathcal{J}^{\omega^{n+1}_{N}}_{f}-\mathcal{J}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}\Big)\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}+\frac{1}{2}(\Delta t)^{3}\int_{\Gamma}(\boldsymbol{u}-(\Delta t)^{-1}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\delta})\cdot\boldsymbol{n}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{N}\cdot\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle+2\nu(\Delta t)^{3}\int_{\Omega_{f}}\mathcal{J}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}\boldsymbol{D}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}(\boldsymbol{u}):\boldsymbol{D}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}(\boldsymbol{v})+(\Delta t)^{2}\int_{\Gamma}\left(\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{N}-p\right)(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\delta}-(\Delta t)\boldsymbol{v})\cdot\boldsymbol{n}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}

\displaystyle+\beta(\Delta t)^{2}\int_{\Gamma}\Big(\mathcal{S}^{\omega}_{\Gamma}\Big)^{-1}\left((\Delta t)^{-1}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\delta}-\boldsymbol{u}\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\delta}-(\Delta t)\boldsymbol{v})\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}+\rho_{b}\int_{\Omega_{b}}\boldsymbol{\eta}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}+h\int_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\delta}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\delta}

\displaystyle+(2\mu_{e}(\Delta t)^{2}+2\mu_{v}(\Delta t))\int_{\Omega_{b}}\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{\eta}):\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{\psi})+(\lambda_{e}(\Delta t)^{2}+\lambda_{v}(\Delta t))\int_{\Omega_{b}}(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\eta})(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi})

\displaystyle-\alpha(\Delta t)^{2}\int_{\Omega_{b}}\mathcal{J}^{(\eta^{n}_{N})^{\delta}}_{b}p\nabla^{(\eta^{n}_{N})^{\delta}}_{b}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\delta}+c_{0}(\Delta t)^{2}\int_{\Omega_{b}}pr-\alpha(\Delta t)^{2}\int_{\Omega_{b}}\mathcal{J}^{(\eta^{n}_{N})^{\delta}}_{b}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\delta}\cdot\nabla^{(\eta^{n}_{N})^{\delta}}_{b}r

\displaystyle-\alpha(\Delta t)^{2}\int_{\Gamma}(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\delta}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}^{\omega^{n}_{N}})r+\kappa(\Delta t)^{3}\int_{\Omega_{b}}\mathcal{J}^{(\eta^{n}_{N})^{\delta}}_{b}\nabla^{(\eta^{n}_{N})^{\delta}}_{b}p\cdot\nabla^{(\eta^{n}_{N})^{\delta}}_{b}r-(\Delta t)^{3}\int_{\Gamma}[(\boldsymbol{u}-(\Delta t)^{-1}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\delta})\cdot\boldsymbol{n}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}]r.

(58)

Note that the influence of the kinematic coupling regularization appears in several terms in the weak formulation, through additional convolution terms involving the convolution parameter
\delta
. However, the terms in which these additional convolutions appear are all consistent in the sense that the resulting bilinear form for the Biot-fluid subproblem is
still coercive
, which reflects the fact that the influence of the kinematic regularization appears consistently throughout the weak formulation in such a way that there is still a well-defined energy estimate. In particular,

B[(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\eta},p),(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\eta},p)]=\frac{1}{2}(\Delta t)^{2}\int_{\Omega_{f}}\left(\mathcal{J}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}+\mathcal{J}^{\omega^{n+1}_{N}}_{f}\right)|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}+2\nu(\Delta t)^{3}\int_{\Omega_{f}}\mathcal{J}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}|\boldsymbol{D}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}(\boldsymbol{u})|^{2}\\
+\beta(\Delta t)\int_{\Gamma}\Big(\mathcal{S}^{\omega}_{\Gamma}\Big)^{-1}|(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\delta}-(\Delta t)\boldsymbol{u})\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}|^{2}+\rho_{b}\int_{\Omega_{b}}|\boldsymbol{\eta}|^{2}+h\int_{\Gamma}|\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\delta}|^{2}+(2\mu_{e}(\Delta t)^{2}+2\mu_{v}(\Delta t))\int_{\Omega_{b}}|\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{\eta})|^{2}\\
+(\lambda_{e}(\Delta t)^{2}+\lambda_{v}(\Delta t))\int_{\Omega_{b}}|\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\eta}|^{2}+c_{0}(\Delta t)^{2}\int_{\Omega_{b}}p^{2}+\kappa(\Delta t)^{3}\int_{\Omega_{b}}\mathcal{J}^{(\eta^{n}_{N})^{\delta}}|\nabla^{(\eta^{n}_{N})^{\delta}}_{b}p|^{2}.

(59)

Therefore, the existence of a unique solution
(\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1}_{N},\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n+1}_{N},p^{n+1}_{N})\in V^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}\times V_{d}\times V_{p}
satisfying the weak formulation is guaranteed by the Lax-Milgram lemma and Korn’s inequality (see Proposition 6.1 in
[
48
]
), which allows us to find a solution to the Biot-fluid subproblem, as long as the bilinear form defined above is coercive. This will be true under the following non-degeneracy assumptions.

Proposition 5.1
.

Suppose that the following geometric non-degeneracy assumptions are satisfied for a fixed but arbitrary
N
and
0\leq n\leq N-1
:

1.

Non-degeneracy of the moving interface.
The map
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{\Gamma}:\hat{\Gamma}\to\Gamma(t)
is injective, and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{f}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}(\hat{\Gamma})\cap\partial\hat{\Omega}=\varnothing
. Furthermore, there exists a positive constant
\alpha>0
(independent of
n
) such that

\|(-\sin(z),\cos(z))+\partial_{z}\boldsymbol{\omega}^{n}_{N}(z)\|\geq\alpha,\qquad\text{ for all }z\in[0,2\pi].

2.

Invertibility of the regularized Lagrangian map.
There exists positive constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
such that

\text{det}\Big(\boldsymbol{I}+\nabla(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n}_{N})^{\delta}\Big)\geq c_{1}>0\ \ \text{ and }\ \ |\boldsymbol{I}+\nabla(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n}_{N})^{\delta}|\leq c_{2}\quad\text{ on }\overline{\Omega_{b}}.

3.

Non-degeneracy of the (fluid domain) ALE map.
There exists a positive constant
c_{3}>0
such that

\mathcal{J}^{\omega^{n+1}_{N}}_{f}\geq c_{3}>0,\quad\mathcal{J}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}\geq c_{3}>0,\quad\text{ on }\overline{\Omega_{b}}.

Then, there exists a unique solution
(\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1}_{N},\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n+1}_{N},\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n+1}_{N},p^{n+1}_{N})
to the weak formulation of the fluid/Biot subproblem in (
54
) and (
55
).

Proof.

Under the assumptions, the existence of a solution to the fluid-Biot problem follows from a usual application of the Lax-Milgram theorem to the bilinear form
B
defined in (
5.1
), which as a result of the geometric assumptions, is coercive due to the calculation in (
59
) and the observation using (
21
) and (
22
) that

\kappa(\Delta t)^{3}\int_{\Omega_{b}}\mathcal{J}^{(\eta^{n}_{N})^{\delta}}|\nabla_{b}^{(\eta^{n}_{N})^{\delta}}p|^{2}\geq\kappa(\Delta t)^{3}c_{1}\int_{\Omega_{b}}|\boldsymbol{I}+\nabla(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n}_{N})^{\delta}|^{-2}|\nabla p|^{2}\geq\kappa(\Delta t)^{3}c_{1}c_{2}^{-2}\int_{\Omega_{b}}|\nabla p|^{2}.

(60)

Furthermore, the discrete Lagrangian map
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}^{(\eta^{n}_{N})^{\delta}}_{b}:\hat{\Omega}_{b}\to(\Omega_{b})^{n,\delta}_{N}
is bijective by Proposition
3.2
and the given assumption on the non-degeneracy of the interface and the regularized Lagrangian map. From this point, the rest of the proof of existence/uniqueness for the fluid-Biot subproblem proceeds as in the proof of Lemma 6.2 in
[
48
]
.
∎

We further obtain the following energy estimate for the fluid/Biot subproblem by substituting
(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{\varphi},\boldsymbol{\psi},r)=(\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1}_{N},\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n+1}_{N},\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n+1}_{N},p^{n+1}_{N})
in the weak formulation for the Biot-fluid subproblem:

\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega_{f}}\mathcal{J}^{\omega^{n+1}_{N}}_{f}|\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1}_{N}|^{2}+2\nu\int_{\Omega_{f}}\mathcal{J}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}|\boldsymbol{D}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1}_{N})|^{2}+\beta\int_{\Gamma}\Big(\mathcal{S}^{\omega}_{\Gamma}\Big)^{-1}\left|\left(\left(\dot{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{n+1}_{N}\right)^{\delta}-\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1}_{N}\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}\right|^{2}\\
+\frac{1}{2}\rho_{b}\int_{\Omega_{b}}|\dot{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{n+1}_{N}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}h\int_{\Gamma}|\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n+1}_{N}|^{2}+\mu_{e}\int_{\Omega_{b}}|\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n+1}_{N})|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{e}\int_{\Omega_{b}}|\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n+1}_{N}|^{2}\\
+2\mu_{v}\int_{\Omega_{b}}|\boldsymbol{D}(\dot{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{n+1}_{N})|^{2}+\lambda_{v}\int_{\Omega_{b}}|\nabla\cdot\dot{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{n+1}_{N}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}c_{0}\int_{\Omega_{b}}|p^{n+1}_{N}|^{2}+\kappa\int_{\Omega_{b}}\mathcal{J}^{(\eta^{n}_{N})^{\delta}}_{b}\left|\nabla^{(\eta^{n}_{N})^{\delta}}_{b}p^{n+1}_{N}\right|^{2}\\
=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega_{f}}\mathcal{J}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}|\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{N}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\rho_{b}\int_{\Omega_{b}}|\dot{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{n}_{N}|^{2}+\mu_{e}\int_{\Omega_{b}}|\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n}_{N})|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{e}\int_{\Omega_{b}}|\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n}_{N}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Gamma}|\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n}_{N}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}c_{0}\int_{\Omega_{b}}|p^{n}_{N}|^{2}.

(61)

5.2
Uniform estimates and approximate solutions

Adding the weak formulations for the plate and the Biot-fluid subproblems, we obtain the following semidiscrete formulation, which is satisfied for all test functions
(\boldsymbol{v},\varphi,\boldsymbol{\psi},r)\in V^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}\times H^{2}(\Gamma)\times V_{s}\times V_{p}
such that
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\delta}|_{\Gamma}=\boldsymbol{\varphi}
:

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{f}}\mathcal{J}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}\boldsymbol{\dot{u}}^{n+1}_{N}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}+2\nu\int_{\Omega_{f}}\mathcal{J}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}\boldsymbol{D}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1}_{N}):\boldsymbol{D}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}(\boldsymbol{v})+\int_{\Gamma}\left(\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1}_{N}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{N}-p^{n+1}_{N}\right)(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\delta}-\boldsymbol{v})\cdot\boldsymbol{n}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}

(62)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega_{f}}\mathcal{J}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}\left[\left(\left(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{N}-\boldsymbol{w}^{n+1}_{N}\right)\cdot\nabla^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1}_{N}\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{v}-\left(\left(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{N}-\boldsymbol{w}^{n+1}_{N}\right)\cdot\nabla^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}\boldsymbol{v}\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1}_{N}\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega_{f}}\left(\frac{\mathcal{J}^{\omega^{n+1}_{N}}_{f}-\mathcal{J}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}}{\Delta t}\right)\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1}_{N}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Gamma}\left(\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1}_{N}-\left(\boldsymbol{\dot{\eta}}^{n+1}_{N}\right)^{\delta}\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{n}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{N}\cdot\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle+\beta\int_{\Gamma}\Big(\mathcal{S}^{\omega}_{\Gamma}\Big)^{-1}\left(\left(\boldsymbol{\dot{\eta}}^{n+1}_{N}\right)^{\delta}-\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1}_{N}\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\delta}-\boldsymbol{v})\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}+\rho_{b}\int_{\Omega_{b}}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{\dot{\eta}}^{n+1}_{N}-\boldsymbol{\dot{\eta}}^{n}_{N}}{\Delta t}\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}

\displaystyle+h\int_{\Gamma}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n+1}_{N}-\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n}_{N}}{\Delta t}\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{\varphi}+2\mu_{e}\int_{\Omega_{b}}\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n+1}_{N}):\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{\psi})+\lambda_{e}\int_{\Omega_{b}}(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n+1}_{N})(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi})

\displaystyle+2\mu_{v}\int_{\Omega_{b}}\boldsymbol{D}(\dot{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{n+1}_{N}):\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{\psi})+\lambda_{v}\int_{\Omega_{b}}(\nabla\cdot\dot{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{n+1}_{N})(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi})-\alpha\int_{\Omega_{b}}\mathcal{J}^{(\eta^{n}_{N})^{\delta}}_{b}p^{n+1}_{N}\nabla^{(\eta^{n}_{N})^{\delta}}_{b}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\delta}+c_{0}\int_{\Omega_{b}}\frac{p^{n+1}_{N}-p^{n}_{N}}{\Delta t}r

\displaystyle-\alpha\int_{\Omega_{b}}\mathcal{J}^{(\eta^{n}_{N})^{\delta}}_{b}\left(\boldsymbol{\dot{\eta}}^{n+1}_{N}\right)^{\delta}\cdot\nabla^{(\eta^{n}_{N})^{\delta}}_{b}r-\alpha\int_{\Gamma}\left(\left(\boldsymbol{\dot{\eta}}^{n+1}_{N}\right)^{\delta}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}\right)r+\kappa\int_{\Omega_{b}}\mathcal{J}^{(\eta^{n}_{N})^{\delta}}_{b}\nabla^{(\eta^{n}_{N})^{\delta}}_{b}p^{n+1}_{N}\cdot\nabla^{(\eta^{n}_{N})^{\delta}}_{b}r

\displaystyle-\int_{\Gamma}\left[\left(\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1}_{N}-\left(\boldsymbol{\dot{\eta}}^{n+1}_{N}\right)^{\delta}\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{n}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}\right]r+h\int_{\Gamma}\Delta\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}_{N}\cdot\Delta\boldsymbol{\varphi}+h\int_{\Gamma}\Delta\boldsymbol{\omega}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}_{N}\cdot\Delta\boldsymbol{\varphi}=0,

\int_{\Gamma}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{\omega}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}_{N}-\boldsymbol{\omega}^{n-\frac{1}{2}}_{N}}{\Delta t}\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{\phi}=\int_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}_{N}\cdot\boldsymbol{\phi},\qquad\int_{\Gamma}\left(\frac{(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n+1}_{N})^{\delta}-(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n}_{N})^{\delta}}{\Delta t}\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{\phi}=\int_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n+1}_{N}\cdot\boldsymbol{\phi},\\
\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\text{ for all }\boldsymbol{\phi}\in L^{2}(\Gamma).

In addition, we can obtain an energy estimate for the approximate solutions. To do this, we define the discrete energy
E^{n+\frac{i}{2}}_{N}
and the dissipation
D^{n}_{N}
for
n=0,1,...,N
and
i=0,1
as follows:

E^{n+\frac{i}{2}}_{N}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega_{f}}\mathcal{J}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}|\boldsymbol{u}^{n+\frac{i}{2}}_{N}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\rho_{b}\int_{\Omega_{b}}|\dot{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{n+\frac{i}{2}}_{N}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}h\int_{\Gamma}|\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n+\frac{i}{2}}_{N}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}h\int_{\Gamma}|\Delta\boldsymbol{\omega}^{n+\frac{i}{2}}_{N}|^{2}\\
+\mu_{e}\int_{\Omega_{b}}|\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n+\frac{i}{2}}_{N})|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{e}\int_{\Omega_{b}}|\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n+\frac{i}{2}}_{N}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}c_{0}\int_{\Omega_{b}}|p^{n+\frac{i}{2}}_{N}|^{2},

D^{n}_{N}=2\nu\int_{\Omega_{f}}\mathcal{J}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}|\boldsymbol{D}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1}_{N})|^{2}+\beta\int_{\Gamma}\Big(\mathcal{S}^{\omega}_{\Gamma}\Big)^{-1}\left|\left(\left(\dot{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{n+1}_{N}\right)^{\delta}-\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1}_{N}\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}\right|^{2}\\
+2\mu_{v}\int_{\Omega_{b}}|\boldsymbol{D}(\dot{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{n+1}_{N})|^{2}+\lambda_{v}\int_{\Omega_{b}}|\nabla\cdot\dot{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{n+1}_{N}|^{2}+\kappa\int_{\Omega_{b}}\mathcal{J}^{(\eta^{n}_{N})^{\delta}}_{b}\left|\nabla^{(\eta^{n}_{N})^{\delta}}_{b}p^{n+1}_{N}\right|^{2}+h\int_{\Gamma}|\Delta\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}_{N}|^{2}.

We then obtain the following energy estimates, which show that the energy of the approximate solutions is uniformly bounded independently of the time discretization parameter
N
. In addition, we obtain uniform bounds on geometric quantities involved in the problem up until some sufficiently small time
T>0
which is independent of
N
and which depends only on the initial data of the problem. This will be useful for showing non-degeneracy of the regularized Lagrangian map for the Biot domain and of the ALE map for the fluid domain.

Proposition 5.2
.

There exists a sufficiently small time
T>0
,
which is independent of the parameters
N
, such that for all positive integers
N
and
n=0,1,...,N
, there exist positive constants
c_{0}
,
c_{1}
,
c_{2}
, and
c_{3}
such that:

\text{det}(\boldsymbol{I}+\nabla(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n}_{N})^{\delta})\geq c_{0}>0,\quad|(\boldsymbol{I}+\nabla(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n}_{N})^{\delta})^{-1}|\geq c_{1}>0,\quad|\boldsymbol{I}+\nabla(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n}_{N})^{\delta}|\leq c_{2},\quad\text{ on }\overline{\Omega_{b}},

(63)

0<c_{3}^{-1}\leq\mathcal{J}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}\leq c_{3},\quad|\nabla\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}|\leq c_{3},\quad\text{ on }\overline{\Omega_{f}},

(64)

and furthermore, the map
\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{\Gamma}:\Gamma\to\Gamma(t)
is injective, with
\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{\Gamma}(\Gamma)\cap\partial\Omega=\varnothing
, and

\|(-\sin(z),\cos(z))+\partial_{z}\boldsymbol{\omega}^{n}_{N}(z)\|\geq\alpha>0,\quad\text{ for all }z\in\Gamma,

(65)

for all
n=0,1,...,N
and for all positive integers
N
, where the constant
\alpha>0
is independent of
n
and
N
. In addition, there exists a uniform constant
C
that is independent of
N
which depends only on the initial data in the problem, such that

\max_{i=0,1,2}\left(\max_{n=0,1,...,N-1}E^{n+\frac{i}{2}}\right)\leq C,\qquad\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}D^{n}_{N}\leq C,

(66)

and such that we have the following uniform numerical dissipation estimate:

\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\Bigg(\int_{\Omega_{f}}\mathcal{J}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}|\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1}_{N}-\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{N}|^{2}+\rho_{b}\int_{\Omega_{b}}|\dot{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{n+1}_{N}-\dot{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{n}_{N}|^{2}+h\int_{\Gamma}|\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n+1}_{N}-\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}_{N}|+h\int_{\Gamma}|\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}_{N}-\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n}_{N}|^{2}\\
+h\int_{\Gamma}|\Delta\boldsymbol{\omega}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}_{N}-\Delta\boldsymbol{\omega}^{n}_{N}|^{2}+\mu_{e}\int_{\Omega_{b}}|\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n+1}_{N})-\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n}_{N})|^{2}+\lambda_{e}\int_{\Omega_{b}}|\nabla\cdot(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n+1}_{N}-\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n}_{N})|^{2}+c_{0}\int_{\Omega_{b}}|p^{n+1}_{N}-p^{n}_{N}|^{2}\Bigg)\leq C

(67)

with the following explicit energy equalities:

E^{n+\frac{1}{2}}_{N}+\frac{1}{2}h\int_{\Gamma}|\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}_{N}-\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n}_{N}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}h\int_{\Gamma}|\Delta\boldsymbol{\omega}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}_{N}-\Delta\boldsymbol{\omega}^{n}_{N}|^{2}=E^{n}_{N},

E^{n+1}_{N}+D^{n}_{N}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega_{f}}\mathcal{J}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}|\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1}_{N}-\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{N}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\rho_{b}\int_{\Omega_{b}}\left|\dot{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{n+1}_{N}-\dot{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{n}_{N}\right|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}h\int_{\Gamma}|\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n+1}_{N}-\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}_{N}|^{2}\\
+\mu_{e}\int_{\Omega_{b}}|\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n+1}_{N}-\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n}_{N})|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{e}\int_{\Omega_{b}}|\nabla\cdot(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n+1}_{N}-\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n}_{N})|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}c_{0}\int_{\Omega_{b}}|p^{n+1}_{N}-p^{n}_{N}|^{2}=E^{n+\frac{1}{2}}_{N}.

Proof.

The semidiscrete energy estimates follow by combining the semidiscrete energy estimates in the plate subproblem (
53
) and the Biot/fluid subproblem (
61
). Therefore, it remains to prove the uniform geometric estimates, which are uniform in
N
. By the uniform energy estimates, note that there exists some uniform constant
C
, independent of
N
, such that

(\Delta t)h\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{i+\frac{1}{2}}_{N}\|^{2}_{H^{2}(\Gamma)}\leq C.

(68)

Therefore, using the fact from (
51
) in the plate subproblem that
\boldsymbol{\omega}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}_{N}-\boldsymbol{\omega}^{n-\frac{1}{2}}_{N}=(\Delta t)\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}_{N}
and the fact from the Biot/fluid subproblem that
\boldsymbol{\omega}^{n+1}_{N}=\boldsymbol{\omega}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}_{N}
, we obtain:

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{\omega}^{n}_{N}-\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0}\|_{C^{1}(\Gamma)}\leq C\|\boldsymbol{\omega}^{n}_{N}-\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0}\|_{H^{2}(\Gamma)}

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}\|\boldsymbol{\omega}^{i+1}_{N}-\boldsymbol{\omega}^{i}_{N}\|_{H^{2}(\Gamma)}\leq C(\Delta t)\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{i+\frac{1}{2}}_{N}\|_{H^{2}(\Gamma)}

\displaystyle\leq C(\Delta t)N^{1/2}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{i+\frac{1}{2}}_{N}\|_{H^{2}(\Gamma)}^{2}\right)^{1/2}\leq CT^{1/2},

(69)

since
N^{1/2}(\Delta t)^{1/2}=T^{1/2}
. Note that the constant in the final estimate (
5.2
) potentially depends on
h
. Then, by taking
T
sufficiently small, using assumptions on the initial data, and applying Proposition
3.1
, we obtain the desired statements about the injectivity and non-degeneracy of the map
\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{\Gamma}
.

The statements about uniform geometric non-degeneracy of the Lagrangian map for the Biot medium follow similarly as in the proof of Lemma 7.2 in
[
48
]
, so it remains to prove the corresponding statement for the ALE maps for the fluid domain. For this purpose, we observe from our previous estimate that

\|\boldsymbol{\omega}^{n}_{N}-\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0}\|_{H^{2}(\Gamma)}\leq CT^{1/2},

for all
n=0,1,...,N
and for all
N
, for a constant
C
that is independent of
N
(which may potentially depend on
h
, which is fixed for now). By the definition of the ALE map in (
30
) and (
31
), and regularity properties of elliptic equations (see Section 5 in
[
42
]
), we obtain that

\|\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}-\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\omega_{0}}_{f}\|_{C^{1}(\Omega_{f})}\leq C\|\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}-\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\omega_{0}}_{f}\|_{H^{5/2}(\Omega_{f})}\leq C\|\boldsymbol{\omega}^{n}_{N}-\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0}\|_{H^{2}(\Gamma)}\leq CT^{1/2},

so since the initial data satisfies
\mathcal{J}^{\omega_{0}}_{f}:=\text{det}(\nabla\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\omega_{0}}_{f})>0
and
|\nabla\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\omega_{0}}_{f}|\leq C
for some positive constant
C
on
\overline{\Omega}_{f}
, the desired assertion on the fluid domain geometric quantities in (
64
) follows by continuity.
∎

Remark 5.2

(A remark about dependence on the plate thickness
h>0
)
.

Due to the estimate in (
68
), the constants
C
in the preceding proof all implicitly depend on the plate thickness
h>0
. At first, this seems to imply that the time of existence of a solution depends on
h
, even though it is explicitly stated in Theorem
4.1
that the time of existence is independent of
h
. However, we will see that this dependence on
h
is a technicality in the scheme, that vanishes in the limit as
N\to\infty
when we obtain the continuous-in-time problem. In particular, the difference of the discrete plate displacement
\boldsymbol{\omega}^{n}_{N}
is determined by the plate velocities
\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}_{N}
obtained in the plate subproblem, which may be different from the plate velocities
\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n+1}_{N}
obtained in the Biot/fluid subproblem. However, in the limit as
N\to\infty
, these plate velocities coincide, and therefore, we can obtain estimates on the plate velocity
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
directly from the trace of the regularized Biot velocity
\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\delta}
, and the estimates on the Biot velocity are
independent of
h
, since they arise directly from the energy of the Biot medium, not from the energy of the plate (which depends on
h
). Thus, we can obtain estimates in the spirit of Proposition
3.1
which are independent of the plate thickness
h>0
in the continuous limit as
N\to\infty
, which gives a (uniform) time of existence that is independent of
h
.

Approximate solutions.
We have approximate solutions
\left(\boldsymbol{u}^{n+\frac{i}{2}}_{N},p^{n+\frac{i}{2}}_{N},\boldsymbol{\omega}^{n+\frac{i}{2}}_{N},\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n+\frac{i}{2}}_{N},\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n+\frac{i}{2}}_{N},\boldsymbol{\xi}^{n+\frac{i}{2}}_{N}\right)
on each time step
[n\Delta t,(n+1)\Delta t]
, and we will now use piecewise constant extensions and (continuous-in-time) linear interpolations to combine these approximate solutions on each individual time step into an approximate solution defined on the full time interval
[0,T]
, for each time discretization parameter
N
. We define an approximate fluid velocity
\boldsymbol{u}_{N}
, Biot displacement
\boldsymbol{\eta}_{N}
, Biot pore pressure
p_{N}
, plate displacement
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{N}
, and plate velocity
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{N}
by taking the value at the right endpoint of each subinterval
(n\Delta t,(n+1)\Delta t]
, namely:

(\boldsymbol{u}_{N}(t),\boldsymbol{\eta}_{N}(t),p_{N}(t),\boldsymbol{\omega}_{N}(t),\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{N}(t))=(\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1}_{N},\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n+1}_{N},p^{n+1}_{N},\boldsymbol{\omega}^{n+1}_{N},\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n+1}_{N}),\quad\text{ for $n\Delta t<t\leq(n+1)\Delta t$}.

(70)

Because the plate velocity is updated after both the plate subproblem and the Biot/fluid subproblem, giving rise to both
\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n}_{N}
and
\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}_{N}
at each time step, we define two versions of the approximate plate velocity since both appear in the semidiscrete formulation: (1) the piecewise constant extension
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{N}
from before defined by

\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{N}(t)=\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n+1}_{N},\quad\text{ for }n\Delta t<t\leq(n+1)\Delta t,

(71)

and (2) a piecewise constant extension
\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{*}_{N}
using the value of the plate velocity at the intermediate “half” steps:

\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{N}^{*}(t)=\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}_{N},\quad\text{ for }n\Delta t<t\leq(n+1)\Delta t.

(72)

Note that these piecewise constant extensions are potentially discontinuous at each
t_{n}=n\Delta t
. Hence, in cases where we need the approximate solutions to be continuous or where we want the approximate solutions to have a weak time derivative, we use the following linear interpolations as approximate solutions:

\overline{\boldsymbol{u}}_{N}(t)=\left(\frac{t-n\Delta t}{\Delta t}\right)\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{N}+\left(\frac{(n+1)\Delta t-t}{\Delta t}\right)\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1}_{N},\quad\text{ for }n\Delta t\leq t\leq(n+1)\Delta t,

\boldsymbol{\overline{\zeta}}_{N}(t)=\left(\frac{t-n\Delta t}{\Delta t}\right)\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n}_{N}+\left(\frac{(n+1)\Delta t-t}{\Delta t}\right)\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{n+1}_{N},\quad\text{ for }n\Delta t\leq t\leq(n+1)\Delta t,

\overline{p}_{N}(t)=\left(\frac{t-n\Delta t}{\Delta t}\right)p^{n}_{N}+\left(\frac{(n+1)\Delta t-t}{\Delta t}\right)p^{n+1}_{N},\quad\text{ for }n\Delta t\leq t\leq(n+1)\Delta t,

\overline{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{N}(t)=\left(\frac{t-n\Delta t}{\Delta t}\right)\boldsymbol{\omega}^{n}_{N}+\left(\frac{(n+1)\Delta t-t}{\Delta t}\right)\boldsymbol{\omega}^{n+1}_{N},\quad\text{ for }n\Delta t\leq t\leq(n+1)\Delta t,

\overline{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{N}(t)=\left(\frac{t-n\Delta t}{\Delta t}\right)\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n}_{N}+\left(\frac{(n+1)\Delta t-t}{\Delta t}\right)\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n+1}_{N},\quad\text{ for }n\Delta t\leq t\leq(n+1)\Delta t.

From the uniform energy estimate in Proposition
5.2
, we therefore have the following uniform boundedness results for the approximate solutions in the parameter
N
, which also implies weak and weak-star subsequential compactness results for the approximate solutions.

Proposition 5.3
.

For each fixed
h>0
and
\delta>0
, we have the following uniform bounds in the time discretization parameter
N
for the approximate solutions:

•

\{\boldsymbol{u}_{N}\}_{N=1}^{\infty}
is uniformly bounded in
L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega_{f}(t)))
and
L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega_{f}(t)))
.

•

\{\boldsymbol{\eta}_{N}\}_{N=1}^{\infty}
is uniformly bounded in
L^{\infty}(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega_{b}))
and
\{\overline{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{N}\}_{N=1}^{\infty}
is uniformly bounded in
L^{\infty}(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega_{b}))
and
W^{1,\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega_{b}))
.

•

\{p_{N}\}_{N=1}^{\infty}
is uniformly bounded in
L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega_{b}))
and
L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega_{b}))
.

•

\{\boldsymbol{\omega}_{N}\}_{N=1}^{\infty}
is uniformly bounded in
L^{\infty}(0,T;H^{2}(\Gamma))
and
\{\overline{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{N}\}_{N=1}^{\infty}
is uniformly bounded in
L^{\infty}(0,T;H^{2}(\Gamma))
,
W^{1,\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Gamma))
, and
H^{1}(0,T;H^{2}(\Gamma))
.

Hence, along a subsequence in
N
(which is still denoted by
N
for notational simplicity), we have the following convergences to limiting functions
\boldsymbol{\eta}
,
p
, and
\boldsymbol{\omega}
:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\eta}_{N}\rightharpoonup\boldsymbol{\eta},\qquad

\displaystyle\text{ weakly-star in $L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega_{b}))$},

\displaystyle p_{N}\rightharpoonup p,\qquad

\displaystyle\text{ weakly-star in $L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega_{b}))$ and weakly in $L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega_{b}))$},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\omega}_{N}\rightharpoonup\boldsymbol{\omega},\qquad

\displaystyle\text{ weakly-star in $L^{\infty}(0,T;H^{2}(\Gamma))$}.

Proof.

These are all immediate from (
66
), and we hence comment only on the uniform bound of
p_{N}
in
L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega_{b}))
. From the uniform energy estimates, we have that for a constant
C
independent of
N
,

\kappa\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\int_{\Omega_{b}}\mathcal{J}^{(\eta^{n}_{N})^{\delta}}_{b}\Big|\nabla^{(\eta^{n}_{N})^{\delta}}_{b}p^{n+1}_{N}\Big|^{2}\leq C.

We can then deduce a bound for
\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\int_{\Omega_{b}}|\nabla p|^{2}
using a similar calculation, as in (
60
), along with the uniform geometric bounds in (
63
).
∎

In addition, we can obtain the following uniform bound on the trace of the fluid velocities, which will be needed for estimating certain terms in the semidiscrete weak formulation. We note that this is not an immediate consequence of a trace inequality, since the fluid velocities
\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1}_{N}
are defined on varying spatial domains
\Omega^{n}_{f,N}:=\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}(\Omega_{f})
.

Proposition 5.4
.

The traces of the approximate fluid velocities
\boldsymbol{u}_{N}|_{\Gamma}
along
\Gamma
are uniformly bounded in
L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Gamma))
.

Proof.

We will establish this uniform bound by using the ALE map to map the fluid velocities
\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1}_{N}
defined on the moving approximate fluid domains
\Omega^{n}_{f,N}:=\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}(\Omega_{f})
back to the reference fluid domain
\Omega_{f}
. We define the following transformed fluid velocities on the reference fluid domain
\Omega_{f}
, which map the fluid velocities
\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1}_{N}
back to the reference domain via the ALE map:

\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}^{n+1}_{N}(t,\hat{x},\hat{y})=\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1}_{N}\Big(t,\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}(\hat{x},\hat{y})\Big).

We claim that the function

\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{N}=\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}^{n+1}_{N}\cdot 1_{[n\Delta t,(n+1)\Delta t)}(t)

(73)

is uniformly bounded in
L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega_{f}))
, where we emphasize that
\Omega_{f}
is now the fixed reference fluid domain, as the desired result on the traces would follow immediately from the trace inequality applied to the reference domain. By the uniform bounds on the Jacobian of the fluid domain ALE map in (
64
), it is clear that
\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{N}
is uniformly bounded in
L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega_{f}))
given the uniform bound of
\boldsymbol{u}_{N}
in
L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega_{f}(t)))
. By (
32
), we can further estimate that

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{f}}|\nabla\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{N}|^{2}\leq C\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{f}(t)}|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{N}|^{2}|\nabla\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\omega}_{f}|^{2}\leq C\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{f}(t)}|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{N}|^{2},

for a constant
C
that is independent of
N
, due to the uniform fluid domain bounds in (
64
).
∎

Because we only obtain weak and weak-star convergences from these uniform boundedness results, we need compactness arguments to obtain strong convergence of approximate solutions in appropriate function spaces, in order to pass to the limit in the nonlinear terms in the approximate weak formulations. This will be the goal of the next subsections.

5.3
Compactness arguments as
N\to\infty

Compactness for the approximate Biot/plate displacements.
Using the classical Arzela-Ascoli compactness results and the uniform bounds of
\overline{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{N}
in the function spaces
L^{\infty}(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega_{b}))
and
W^{1,\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega_{b}))
and of
\boldsymbol{\overline{\omega}}_{N}
in
L^{\infty}(0,T;H^{2}(\Gamma))
and
W^{1,\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Gamma))
from Proposition
5.3
, we have the following strong convergence:

Proposition 5.5
.

Along a subsequence,
\overline{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{N}\to\boldsymbol{\eta}
in
C(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega_{b}))
and
\overline{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{N}\to\boldsymbol{\omega}
in
C(0,T;H^{s}(\Gamma))
for any
0<s<2
.

Proof.

The convergence of
\boldsymbol{\eta}_{N}
in
C(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega_{b}))
and
\overline{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{N}
in
C(0,T;H^{s}(\Gamma))
follows from Arzela-Ascoli, once we use interpolation to deduce a uniform bound of
\overline{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{N}
in
C^{\alpha}(0,T;H^{s}(\Gamma))
for each
0<s<2
, for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
depending on
s\in(0,2)
. From the uniform dissipation estimates in (
67
) and the uniform bounds in (
66
),

\displaystyle(\Delta t)\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}

\displaystyle\Big(\|\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n+1}_{N}-\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n}_{N}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{b})}^{2}+\|\nabla(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n+1}_{N}-\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n}_{N})\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{b})}^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq(\Delta t)^{3}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\|\boldsymbol{\xi}^{n+1}_{N}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{b})}^{2}+(\Delta t)\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\|\nabla(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n+1}_{N}-\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n}_{N})\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{b})}^{2}\leq C\Delta t(1+(\Delta t)).

Hence,
\|\overline{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{N}-\boldsymbol{\eta}_{N}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega_{b}))}\to 0
(and by a similar argument,
\|\overline{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{N}-\boldsymbol{\omega}_{N}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;H^{2}(\Gamma))}\to 0
), as
N\to\infty
, so the strong limits of
\overline{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{N}
and
\overline{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{N}
in this proposition coincide with the weak and weak-star limits of
\boldsymbol{\eta}_{N}
and
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{N}
in Proposition
5.3
.
∎
