satisfy the following weak formulation for all test functions
(\boldsymbol{\psi},\boldsymbol{\varphi})\in\mathcal{Q}_{v}
and for all times
0\leq s\leq t\leq T
:

\rho_{b}\int_{\Omega_{b}}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h}(t)-\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h}(s))\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}+h\int_{\Gamma}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{h}(t)-\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{h}(s))\cdot\boldsymbol{\varphi}=\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Gamma}\left(p_{h}-\frac{1}{2}|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}|^{2}\right)(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\delta}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}^{\omega_{h}})\\
+\beta\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Gamma}\Big(\mathcal{S}^{\omega_{h}}_{\Gamma}\Big)^{-1}(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}-\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{h})\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}^{\omega_{h}}\left(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\delta}\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}^{\omega_{h}}\right)-2\mu_{e}\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Omega_{b}}\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{h}):\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{\psi})-\lambda_{e}\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Omega_{b}}(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\eta}_{h})(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi})\\
-2\mu_{v}\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Omega_{b}}\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h}):\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{\psi})-2\lambda_{v}\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Omega_{b}}(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h})(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi})+\alpha\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Omega_{b}}\mathcal{J}^{\eta^{\delta}_{h}}_{b}p_{h}\nabla^{\eta^{\delta}_{h}}_{b}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\delta}+h\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Gamma}\Delta\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{h}\cdot\Delta\boldsymbol{\varphi}+h\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Gamma}\Delta\boldsymbol{\omega}_{h}\cdot\Delta\boldsymbol{\varphi}.

We have the following convergence result for the Biot velocity and interface velocity.

Proposition 6.7
.

Along a subsequence,
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h}\to\boldsymbol{\xi}
in
L^{2}(0,T;H^{-s}(\Omega_{b}))
for
-1<s<0
and
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{h}\to\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\delta}|_{\Gamma}
in
L^{2}(0,T;H^{k}(\Gamma))
for all positive integers
k
. Furthermore, we can explicitly identify
\boldsymbol{\xi}=\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\eta}
.

Proof.

Consider the ordered pair of Biot velocities and plate velocities
(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h},h\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{h})
in the parameter
h>0
, where we multiply the plate velocity by
h
due to the weak formulation above. Note that
(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h},h\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{h})
are uniformly bounded independently of
h
in
L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega_{b})\times L^{2}(\Gamma))
by Proposition
6.2
(and in fact
\|h\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{h}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Gamma))}\to 0
as
h\to 0
). Furthermore, from the weak formulation, we can show that
(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h},h\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{h})
is uniformly bounded in
W^{\alpha,2}(0,T;\mathcal{Q}_{v}^{\prime})
for an appropriate choice of
\alpha>0
, by establishing an increment estimate. To do this, we estimate each of the terms of the weak formulation for the Biot and plate velocity for a test function
(\boldsymbol{\psi},\boldsymbol{\varphi})\in\mathcal{Q}_{v}
, with
\|\boldsymbol{\psi}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega_{b})}\leq 1
and
\|\boldsymbol{\varphi}\|_{H^{2}_{0}(\Gamma)}\leq 1
, as follows:

•

Note that by the trace inequality,
p_{h}
is uniformly bounded in
L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Gamma))
and by the trace inequality combined with the properties of spatial convolution,
\|\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\delta}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma)}\leq C
. Furthermore, we have the estimate
|\boldsymbol{n}^{\omega_{h}}|\leq C
uniformly in
h
, where the rescaled normal vector is defined in (
50
). We estimate:

\displaystyle\left|\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Gamma}\left(p_{h}-\frac{1}{2}|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}|^{2}\right)(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\delta}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}^{\omega^{\delta}_{h}})\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{s}^{t}\left(\|p_{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{s}^{t}\left(\|p_{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}+\|\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{h}\|^{2}_{H^{3/4}(\Omega_{f})}\right)\leq C|t-s|^{1/4},

(101)

where we used the fact that
\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{h}:=\boldsymbol{u}_{h}\circ\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\omega_{h}}_{f}\in L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega_{f}))\cap L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega_{f}))
and hence is in
L^{8/3}(0,T;H^{3/4}(\Omega_{f}))
uniformly in
h
, by the estimates in Proposition
6.3
and the geometric bounds in Proposition
6.1
.

•

Next, we use the facts that
|\boldsymbol{\tau}^{\omega^{\delta}_{h}}|\leq C
uniformly in
h
where the rescaled tangent vector is defined in (
50
), the geometric estimate
\mathcal{J}^{\omega^{\delta}_{h}}_{\Gamma}\geq C
by the definition in (
38
) and the uniform bound (
94
), the assumption on the test function that
\|\boldsymbol{\psi}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega_{f})}\leq 1
, and the trace inequality to obtain:

\left|\beta\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Gamma}\Big(\mathcal{S}^{\omega_{h}}_{\Gamma}\Big)^{-1}(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}-\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{h})\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}^{\omega_{h}}\left(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\delta}\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}^{\omega_{h}}\right)\right|\\
\leq C\int_{s}^{t}\left(\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}\right)\leq C\int_{s}^{t}\left(\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}+\|\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{h}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega_{f})}\right)\leq C|t-s|^{1/2},

by the uniform bound of
\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{h}
in
L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega_{f}))
in Proposition
6.3
and the uniform bound of
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{h}
in
L^{2}(0,T;H^{k}(\Gamma))
for all positive integers
k
, see Proposition
6.2
.

•

Next, we estimate using the uniform pointwise geometric bounds on
\mathcal{J}^{\eta^{\delta}_{h}}_{b}
and
|(\boldsymbol{I}+\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\delta}_{h})^{-1}|
in Proposition
6.1
, that

\left|\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Omega_{b}}\mathcal{J}^{\eta^{\delta}_{h}}_{b}p_{h}\nabla^{\eta^{\delta}_{h}}_{b}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\delta}\right|\leq C\int_{s}^{t}\|p_{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{b})}\leq C|t-s|.

Hence, we deduce the following increment estimate:

\|(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h},h\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{h})(t)-(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h},h\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{h})(s)\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{v}^{\prime}}\leq C|t-s|^{1/4},

and by the definition of the fractional Sobolev space in (
98
), for any
\alpha
such that
0<\alpha<1/8
, we obtain that

\|(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h},h\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{h})\|_{W^{\alpha,2}(0,T;\mathcal{Q}_{v}^{\prime})}\leq C,\quad\text{ uniformly in $h$}.

Therefore, using the compactness result in Proposition
6.4
along with the chain of embeddings
L^{2}(\Omega_{b})\times L^{2}(\Gamma)\subset\subset H^{-s}(\Omega_{b})\times H^{-s}(\Gamma)\subset\mathcal{Q}_{v}^{\prime}
, we obtain that
(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h},h\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{h})
converges strongly in
L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega_{b})\times L^{2}(\Gamma))
to some limiting ordered pair of functions.

We will hence denote the limit of
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h}
in
L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega_{b}))
by
\boldsymbol{\xi}
, and since
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h}\rightharpoonup\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\eta}
weakly-star in
L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega_{b}))
by the weak-star convergence of
\boldsymbol{\eta}_{h}\rightharpoonup\boldsymbol{\eta}
in
W^{1,\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega_{b}))
, we must have that
\boldsymbol{\xi}=\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\eta}
by uniqueness properties of the limit. By the regularized kinematic coupling condition,
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{h}=\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h}^{\delta}|_{\Gamma}
, so by the regularizing properties of spatial convolution and the trace inequality, we obtain from the strong convergence
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h}\to\boldsymbol{\xi}
in
L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Gamma))
that
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{h}\to\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\delta}|_{\Gamma}
in
L^{2}(0,T;H^{k}(\Gamma))
for any positive integer
k
.
∎

Compactness arguments for the fluid velocity.
For the fluid velocities, we must handle the issue of moving domains when obtaining strong convergence results as in Proposition
5.8
, since the approximate fluid velocities
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}
are all defined on different moving fluid domains. However, this will be quite simple compared to the semidiscrete case in the existence proof for the
h
-level problem in Proposition
5.8
. The main difficulty in the case of the semidiscrete scheme in Proposition
5.8
is that the time-discrete fluid velocities satisfy different weak formulations on each time interval, due to the interface update being staggered from the fluid/Biot update, which leads to requiring us to estimate the difference between consecutive ALE fluid maps
\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\omega^{n}_{N}}_{f}
and
\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\omega^{n+1}_{N}}_{f}
, see (
76
) and (
77
). However, because our approximate solutions
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}
in the limit passage as
h\to 0
are time-continuous (rather than discretized in time), the compactness arguments for the fluid velocity are simpler, as we will be able to use an extension to by zero to express the weak formulation for
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}
on a common maximal fluid domain, where we can use an increment estimate and compactness via fractional Sobolev spaces (Proposition
6.4
) to conclude strong convergence.

To do this, we extend the fluid velocities
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}
from
\Omega_{h}(t):=\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\omega_{h}(t)}_{f}(\Omega_{f})
to the fixed maximal domain
\Omega:=\{\boldsymbol{x}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}:|\boldsymbol{x}|<2\}
, as was done in the proof of existence for fixed
h
when passing to the limit as
N\to\infty
, see the discussion preceding Proposition
5.9
and its statement. This is possible due to the non-degeneracy results on
\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\omega_{h}}_{f}(\Gamma)
stated in Proposition
6.1
. Because the fluid velocity in particular is decoupled from the plate velocity, we can treat the fluid velocities as functions on the maximal domain and directly estimate the fluid velocities in a dual norm for a fixed function space, in order to appeal to the compactness result in Proposition
6.4
to directly obtain strong convergence. We emphasize that having the fluid velocity function decoupled from the other parts of the solution greatly simplifies this limit procedure, in contrast to for example having the fluid velocity kinematically coupled to the plate velocity via a kinematic coupling condition (such as the no-slip condition), as in past works on FSI in
[
62
,
71
,
63
]
.

We hence obtain the following strong convergence result for the fluid velocities
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}
defined on the fixed maximal fluid domain:

Proposition 6.8
.

Along a subsequence in
h
,
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}\to\boldsymbol{u}
in
L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))
for some limiting function
\boldsymbol{u}
that is equal to zero outside of the limiting moving fluid domain
\Omega_{f}(t)
defined by the regularized interface displacement
\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\delta}|_{\Gamma}
and the corresponding moving interface
\Gamma^{\eta^{\delta}}(t)
.

Proof.

We will appeal to the compactness result in Proposition
6.4
, where we first note that the fluid velocities
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}
extended to the maximal fluid domain
\Omega
are uniformly bounded in
L^{2}(0,T;H^{s}(\Omega))
for any
s
such that
0<s<1/2
, due to the uniform bound of
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}\in L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega_{f})
and properties of extension by zero on Lipschitz domains (see Theorem 8.2 in
[
46
]
). We then define the following common test space on the maximal fluid domain:

\mathcal{Q}_{M}=\{\boldsymbol{v}\in H^{1}(\Omega):\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{v}=0\text{ on }\Omega\text{ and }\boldsymbol{v}=0\text{ on }\partial\Omega_{f}\setminus\Gamma\}.

We claim that the fluid velocities
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}
on the maximal fluid domain are bounded in
W^{\alpha,2}(0,T;\mathcal{Q}_{M}^{\prime})
for some
\alpha>0
, uniformly in the plate thickness parameter
h
. To show this, we note that for any
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathcal{Q}_{M}
, the restriction to the moving fluid domains
1_{\Omega_{f,h}(t)}\boldsymbol{v}
are admissible test functions for the weak formulation with parameter
h>0
, and hence using Definition
5.1
, for any (spacetime) test function
\boldsymbol{v}\in C_{c}^{1}([0,T);\mathcal{Q}_{M})
, the following weak formulation for
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}
holds on the fixed maximal domain:

-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{u}_{h}\cdot\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{v}-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{u}_{h}\cdot(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{v}+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma(t)}\left(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}-\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{h}\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{n}(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}\cdot\boldsymbol{v})+2\nu\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{f}(t)}\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}):\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{v})\\
-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma(t)}\left(\frac{1}{2}|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}|^{2}-p_{h}\right)(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})-\beta\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma(t)}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{h}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h})\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau})=\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}(0).

(102)

This follows by the support properties of the function
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}
and the extension by zero, where we note that we also fully transferred the advective derivative in the second term of (
102
) to the test function using integration by parts. Hence, using the weak continuity of
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}
, we obtain that for all (spatial) test functions
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathcal{Q}_{M}
and for all times
0\leq s\leq t\leq T
:

\int_{\Omega}(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}(t)-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}(s))\cdot\boldsymbol{v}=\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{u}_{h}\cdot(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{v}+\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Gamma(t)}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{h}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h})\cdot\boldsymbol{n}(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}\cdot\boldsymbol{v})\\
-2\nu\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Omega_{f}(t)}\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}):\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{v})+\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Gamma(t)}\left(\frac{1}{2}|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}|^{2}-p_{h}\right)(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})+\beta\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Gamma(t)}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{h}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h})\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}).

We estimate the terms for
\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{Q_{M}}\leq 1
(so that
\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}\leq 1
) as follows:

•

We estimate using geometric bounds on the Jacobian of the fluid ALE map (Proposition
6.1
):

\displaystyle\left|\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{u}_{h}\cdot(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{v}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{s}^{t}\|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}\|_{L^{4}(\Omega_{f}(t))}^{2}\leq C\int_{s}^{t}\|\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{h}\|_{L^{4}(\Omega_{f})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{s}^{t}\|\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{h}\|_{H^{1/2}(\Omega_{f})}^{2}\leq\int_{s}^{t}\left(\|\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{f}(t))}\|\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{h}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega_{f}(t))}\right)\leq C|t-s|^{1/2},

since
\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{h}
is uniformly bounded in
L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega_{f}))\cap L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega_{f}))
by Proposition
6.3
.

•

Next, since
\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega_{f})}\leq 1
, by the trace inequality and Sobolev embedding,
\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{L^{4}(\Gamma)}\leq 1
. Therefore,

\left|\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Gamma(t)}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{h}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h})\cdot\boldsymbol{n}(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}\cdot\boldsymbol{v})\right|\leq C\int_{s}^{t}\left(\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}\|_{L^{4}(\Gamma)}\right)\|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}\|_{L^{4}(\Gamma)}\\
\leq C\int_{s}^{t}\left(1+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}\|_{L^{4}(\Gamma)}^{2}\right)\leq C\int_{s}^{t}\Big(1+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}\|_{H^{1/4}(\Gamma)}^{2}\Big)\leq C\int_{s}^{t}\Big(1+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}\|_{H^{3/4}(\Omega_{f})}^{2}\Big)\leq C|t-s|^{1/4}

since
\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{h}
is uniformly bounded in
L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega_{f}))\cap L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega_{f}))
and hence also in
L^{8/3}(0,T;H^{3/4}(\Omega_{f}))
by Proposition
6.3
. Similarly, by the uniform bound of
p_{h}
in
L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega_{b}))
given in Proposition
6.2
,

\left|\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Gamma(t)}\left(\frac{1}{2}|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}|^{2}-p_{h}\right)(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\right|\leq C\int_{s}^{t}\left(\|p_{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}\|_{L^{4}(\Gamma)}^{2}\right)\leq C|t-s|^{1/4},

and by using the uniform bound of
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{h}
in
L^{2}(0,T;H^{k}(\Gamma))
for all positive integers
k
given in Proposition
6.2
,

\displaystyle\left|\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Gamma(t)}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{h}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h})\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau})\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{s}^{t}\left(\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{s}^{t}\left(\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}\|_{H^{1}(\Gamma)}\right)\leq C|t-s|^{1/2}.

•

Finally, by using the uniform estimate of
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}
in
L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega_{f}(t)))
in Proposition
6.2
, we estimate

\left|2\nu\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Omega_{f}(t)}\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}):\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{v})\right|\leq C|t-s|^{1/2},

So we obtain the final estimate that

\sup_{\|v\|_{Q_{M}}\leq 1}\left|\int_{\Omega}(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}(t)-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}(s))\cdot\boldsymbol{v}\right|\leq C|t-s|^{1/4},

and hence,
\|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}\|_{W^{\alpha,2}(0,T;\mathcal{Q}_{M}^{\prime})}\leq C
uniformly in
h
for any
\alpha
such that
0<\alpha<1/8
by (
98
). The desired strong convergence of
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}\to\boldsymbol{u}
along a subsequence in
h
follows by the compact embedding in Proposition
6.4
. The support properties of the limit
\boldsymbol{u}
follow from the uniform pointwise convergence of
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{h}
to
\boldsymbol{\eta}|_{\Gamma}
and the fact that
L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))
convergence implies convergence for almost every
(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in[0,T]\times\Omega
along a subsequence in
h
, combined with the support properties of the extension by zero of
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}
.
∎

6.2
Passage to the limit

We conclude the proof of the main result Theorem
4.1
by sketching how we can pass to the limit in the weak formulation for the approximate problem with plate of thickness
h>0
in Definition
5.1
to obtain the limiting weak formulation for the regularized interface problem in Definition
4.3
. We summarize the convergences we have obtained so far below, which follow from Proposition
6.5
, Proposition
6.6
, Proposition
6.7
, and an argument similar to Proposition
5.10
. Using these convergences, we can pass to the limit in the regularized interface weak formulations for the approximate problem with a plate of thickness
h>0
(Definition
5.1
) to obtain a regularized interface weak solution to the original direct contact problem (Definition
4.3
).

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
(\boldsymbol{u}_{h},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{h},\boldsymbol{\eta}_{h},p_{h})
be regularized interface weak solutions for the approximate problem with plate of thickness
h>0
in the sense of Definition
5.1
, which all exist on a common time interval
[0,T]
, for the same initial data, satisfying the conditions of Theorem
5.1
. We have the following convergences in the limit as
h\to 0
.

•

\boldsymbol{\eta}_{h}\to\boldsymbol{\eta}
in
C(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega_{b}))
.

•

p_{h}\to p
in
L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega_{b}))
.

•

\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h}\to\boldsymbol{\xi}
in
L^{2}(0,T;H^{-s}(\Omega_{b}))
for
-1<s<0
.

•

\boldsymbol{u}_{h}\to\boldsymbol{u}
in
L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))
on the maximal domain.

•

\boldsymbol{u}_{h}|_{\Gamma}\to\boldsymbol{u}|_{\Gamma}
in
L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Gamma))
.

•

\boldsymbol{\omega}_{h}\to\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\delta}|_{\Gamma}
in
C(0,T;H^{k}(\Gamma))
for all positive integers
k
.

•

\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{h}\to\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\delta}|_{\Gamma}
in
L^{2}(0,T;H^{k}(\Gamma))
for all positive integers
k
.

Furthermore, the limiting solution
(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\eta},p)
is a regularized interface weak solution to the original direct contact problem in the sense of Definition
4.3
.

The passage to the limit from the
h
-level weak formulation to the limiting weak formulation features similar difficulties as to the passage in
N\to\infty
for the approximate problem, in the sense that there are integrals over moving fluid domains. Hence, we can use the same technique of identifying target test functions and constructing test functions that are admissible for each of the approximate
h
-level weak formulations, which converge appropriate to the target test function. We can hence pass to the limit as
h\to 0
in the approximate weak formulations in Definition
5.1
to obtain the limiting weak formulation in Definition
4.3
via the same arguments as in Section
5.4
. We only make comments about the associated plate terms in the weak formulation in Definition
5.1
and how they vanish in the limit as
h\to 0
:

-h\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma}\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\omega}\cdot\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\varphi}+h\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma}\Delta\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\omega}\cdot\Delta\boldsymbol{\varphi}+h\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma}\Delta\boldsymbol{\omega}\cdot\Delta\boldsymbol{\varphi}.

Note that by the uniform bound of
h^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\omega}_{h}
in
L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Gamma))
,
h^{1/2}\Delta\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{h}
in
L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Gamma))
, and
h^{1/2}\Delta\boldsymbol{\omega}_{h}
in
L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Gamma))
, we have that these terms converge to zero, since
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
is a fixed test function. For example,

\left|h\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma}\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\omega}_{h}\cdot\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\varphi}\right|\leq h^{1/2}\|h^{1/2}\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\omega}_{h}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Gamma))}\|\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\varphi}\|_{L^{1}(0,T;L^{2}(\Gamma))}\to 0,

as there is an extra
h^{1/2}
factor, which causes the integral to converge to zero in the limit as
h\to 0
. Thus, we can pass to the limit in the weak formulation for the
h
-level problem to obtain a regularized interface weak solution to the direct contact FPSI problem with nonlinear geometric coupling in the sense of Definition
5.1
, which completes the proof of the main result in Theorem
4.1
.

7
Conclusions

In this manuscript, we introduced a
regularized interface method
to study fluid-poroelastic structure interaction between an incompressible, viscous fluid and a poroelastic medium modeled by the Biot equations. The two systems are nonlinearly coupled across a moving interface defined by the trace of the Biot displacement. We focused on weak, finite-energy solutions to this problem, which is notoriously difficult to analyze due to the limited regularity of the interface. This lack of regularity complicates the rigorous definition of a weak solution: key geometric quantities–such as the Jacobian of the Lagrangian map on the Biot domain–lack sufficient regularity to ensure all terms in the formally derived weak formulation are well-defined.

The regularized interface method developed here is specifically designed to address this issue by providing a framework for defining a weak solution in the presence of low-regularity interface geometry. It consists of two main components: (1) spatial regularization of the Biot displacement to define a smooth, regularized moving interface, and (2) modification of the weak formulation to ensure that the associated energy is consistent with the original, non-regularized problem.

The regularization introduced via a fixed parameter
\delta>0
provides uniform control over higher-order spatial derivatives of the Biot displacement. This, in turn, enables uniform-in-time control of key geometric quantities and it allows us to control the onset of geometric degeneracies–at least locally in time.
Although the regularized interface method was initially developed to address the ill-defined nature of the direct contact interface problems, the additional regularity gained from spatial convolution has a further important benefit: it allows for the treatment of
vector-valued displacements
in the Biot medium. By controlling higher-order norms of the regularized Biot displacement, we can enforce injectivity uniformly in time for the approximate solutions, provided
\delta
remains fixed. We emphasize that the inclusion of
bulk poroelasticity with vector-valued displacements
in the context of weak solutions is a novel aspect of the present work.

We established the existence of a direct contact regularized interface weak solution via an approximation scheme that introduces a plate of thickness
h>0
at the interface and then takes the singular limit as
h\to 0
. Existence for the approximate problem, involving a plate, was proven using a Lie operator splitting approach, which alternates between solving the plate subproblem and the Biot/fluid subproblem (see Section
5
).
We emphasize that the existence analysis is rather technically involved, despite the spatial regularization of the Biot displacement providing uniform control over higher-order norms. In the approximate problem, the interface–modeled as a thin plate–undergoes vector-valued displacements from its reference configuration
\Gamma
. This necessitated the use of a harmonic extension ALE map for the fluid domain (see (
30
) and (
31
)) and required establishing uniform geometric control over mappings from fixed to reference domains (see Proposition
5.2
).
These controls were essential for transitioning between the Lagrangian and Eulerian formulations and for avoiding geometric degeneracy.

Moreover, the more intricate geometry of the vector-valued interface displacements introduced substantial complexity in the compactness analysis. In particular, establishing compactness for the fluid velocities required nontrivial estimates comparing different time-discrete moving fluid domains, which vary due to the moving approximate geometries
\Gamma^{n}_{\Delta t}
. These technical challenges were addressed in Proposition
5.2
.

Next, we addressed passing to the limit as
h\to 0
in the “approximate” problems with a plate of thickness
h
to obtain a limiting regularized interface weak solution for the direct contact problem, as stated in Theorem
6.1
.
A main component of this analysis was again, finding uniform geometric bounds independent of
h
, see Proposition
6.1
, and also showing that the regularized interface weak solutions for the approximate
h
-level problems all exist on a uniform time interval, which was accomplished by energy estimates.
This completed the proof of the main result stated in Theorem
4.1
.

We remark that the regularized interface method introduced here, based on an explicit regularization of the direct contact interface, may initially appear to be a purely technical or artificial tool for mathematical analysis–especially since each choice of regularization parameter
\delta>0
leads to a distinct regularized interface weak solution. However, the preliminary results of our current work indicate that the
\delta
-regularization of the problem introduced here, is a
“good approximation”
to the original, non-regularized problem in the sense that our “approximate” solutions of the
\delta
-regularized problems converge, as
\delta\to 0
to the classical solution of the non-regularized problem when such a solution exists. More precisely, our preliminary results indicate that we will be able to establish
weak-classical consistency
– given a smooth (classical) solution
(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\eta},p)
to the original direct contact Biot-fluid problem that exists on some time interval
[0,T]
, the regularized interface weak solutions
(\boldsymbol{u}_{\delta},\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\delta},p_{\delta})
all exist on that same uniform time interval
[0,T]
, and furthermore, these solutions converge in an appropriate norm as
\delta\to 0
to the smooth (classical) solution
(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\eta},p)
.

We note that for a slightly different regularization scheme applied to a simpler FPSI problem with nonlinear geometric coupling and only transverse interface displacements, involving a separating reticular plate at the interface, a similar weak-classical consistency result has been established in
[
48
]
. Our experience with that analysis and our preliminary results so far give us reason to be optimistic that a similar result can be established for the regularized interface method, applied to the more challenging setting of the direct contact Biot-fluid problem, studied here.

We conclude with the remark that, although the regularized interface method was developed specifically for the direct contact Biot-fluid problem considered in this manuscript, its advantages suggest that it may serve as a robust framework for analyzing a broader class of moving domain problems–particularly those in which the geometry depends on solutions with low regularity. The method’s ability to provide uniform geometric control, even in the presence of vector-valued displacements in bulk poroelasticity, points to its potential applicability to direct contact FSI problem with bulk elasticity, and other systems involving complex geometric nonlinearities. Extending the regularized interface method to such broader settings represents a promising direction for future research.

8
Appendix

Figure 3:
The curves
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
connecting two points
(x_{1},y_{1})
and
(x_{2},y_{2})
in
\Omega_{f}
, where
\gamma_{1}
is purely radial and
\gamma_{2}
is purely circular (constant radius).

In the appendix, we collect some auxiliary results that are used in the analysis. The first is a result related to the (reference) fluid domain geometry
\Omega_{f}:=\{\boldsymbol{x}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}:1<|\boldsymbol{x}|<2\}
. Because
\Omega_{f}
is not convex, as the line segment connecting two points in
\Omega_{f}
is not necessarily contained entirely within
\Omega_{f}
, we must define an alternative notion of “distance” within
\Omega_{f}
that is equivalent to the usual Euclidean norm.

Let
(x_{1},y_{1})
and
(x_{2},y_{2})
be two points in
\Omega_{f}
, with associated polar coordinates
(r_{1},\theta_{1})
and
(r_{2},\theta_{2})
respectively, where
1<r_{i}<2
for
i=1,2
and
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in(-2\pi,2\pi)
are chosen so that
|\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}|<\pi
. (While polar coordinates can be expressed modulo
2\pi
in the angle, we choose representatives
\theta_{1}
and
\theta_{2}
that are the “closest” to each other). Let
\gamma_{1}(x_{1},y_{1},x_{2},y_{2})
and
\gamma_{2}(x_{1},y_{1},x_{2},y_{2})
be the curves parametrized by
0\leq s\leq 1
by

\gamma_{1}(x_{1},y_{1},x_{2},y_{2}):s\to((1-s)r_{1}+sr_{2},\theta_{1}),

\gamma_{2}(x_{1},y_{1},x_{2},y_{2}):(r_{2},(1-s)\theta_{1}+s\theta_{2}),

in polar coordinates. Then, let
\gamma(x_{1},y_{1},x_{2},y_{2})
be the composition of the two curves
\gamma_{1}(x_{1},y_{1},x_{2},y_{2})
followed by
\gamma_{2}(x_{1},y_{1},x_{2},y_{2})
. See Figure
3
. Since
\gamma(x_{1},y_{1},x_{2},y_{2})
is a curve contained entirely within
\Omega_{f}
connecting
(x_{1},y_{1})
and
(x_{2},y_{2})
, its length can be thought of as a natural measure of distance within
\Omega_{f}
. We claim that the length of this connecting curve
\gamma(x_{1},y_{1},x_{2},y_{2})
is equivalent to the usual Euclidean distance.

Proposition 8.1
.

Consider points
(x_{1},y_{1})
and
(x_{2},y_{2})
in
\Omega_{f}
, connected by the curve
\gamma(x_{1},y_{1},x_{2},y_{2})\subset\Omega
defined above. Then,

|(x_{1},y_{1})-(x_{2},y_{2})|\leq\text{Length}\Big(\gamma(x_{1},y_{1},x_{2},y_{2})\Big)\leq 5|(x_{1},y_{1})-(x_{2},y_{2})|,

for all
(x_{1},y_{1}),(x_{2},y_{2})\in\Omega_{f}
.

Proof.

We first show the assertion when
r_{1}=r_{2}
, namely, when
|(x_{1},y_{1})|=|(x_{2},y_{2})|
. In this case, the curve
\gamma(x_{1},y_{1},x_{2},y_{2})
is just the curve
\gamma_{2}(x_{1},y_{1},x_{2},y_{2})
. Without loss of generality, by rotation and reflection, we can assume that
\theta_{1}=0
and
0<\theta_{2}\leq\pi
, in which case, we can compute, where
r
is the common value of
r_{1}=r_{2}
:

\text{Length}\Big(\gamma(x_{1},y_{1},x_{2},y_{2})\Big)=r\theta_{2},

|(x_{1},y_{1})-(x_{2},y_{2})|=|(r,0)-(r\cos(\theta_{2}),r\sin(\theta_{2}))|=r\sqrt{2}\Big(\sqrt{1-\cos(\theta_{2})}\Big).

Since
0<\theta_{2}\leq\pi
, we can estimate
\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{1-\cos(\theta_{2})}}{\theta_{2}}\geq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}
for all
0<\theta_{2}\leq\pi
. Therefore, we conclude that

\text{Length}\Big(\gamma(x_{1},y_{1},x_{2},y_{2})\Big)\leq 2|(x_{1},y_{1})-(x_{2},y_{2})|,\qquad\text{ for all }(x_{1},y_{1}),(x_{2},y_{2})\in\Omega_{f}\\
\text{ with }|(x_{1},y_{1})|=|(x_{2},y_{2})|.

(103)

Now consider general
(x_{1},y_{1})
and
(x_{2},y_{2})
in
\Omega_{f}
and let
(x^{*},y^{*})\in\Omega_{f}
be the intermediate point, namely the endpoint of
\gamma_{1}(x_{1},y_{1},x_{2},y_{2})
and the starting point of
\gamma_{2}(x_{1},y_{1},x_{2},y_{2})
, which has polar coordinates
(r_{2},\theta_{1})
. Then, we calculate that

\text{Length}\Big(\gamma(x_{1},y_{1},x_{2},y_{2})\Big)=\text{Length}\Big(\gamma_{1}(x_{1},y_{1},x_{2},y_{2})\Big)+\text{Length}\Big(\gamma_{2}(x_{1},y_{1},x_{2},y_{2})\Big).

By the definition of
\gamma_{1}(x_{1},y_{1},x_{2},y_{2})
, we estimate that

\text{Length}\Big(\gamma_{1}(x_{1},y_{1},x_{2},y_{2})\Big)=\Big||(x_{1},y_{1})|-|(x_{2},y_{2})|\Big|\leq|(x_{1},y_{1})-(x_{2},y_{2})|.

Since
\gamma_{2}(x_{1},y_{1},x_{2},y_{2})
connects the points
(x^{*},y^{*})
and
(x_{2},y_{2})
along a constant radius, by (
103
),

\displaystyle\text{Length}\Big(\gamma_{2}(x_{1},y_{1},x_{2},y_{2})\Big)

\displaystyle\leq 2\|(x^{*},y^{*})-(x_{2},y_{2})\|

\displaystyle\leq 2\Big(|(x_{1},y_{1})-(x_{2},y_{2})|+|(x^{*},y^{*})-(x_{1},y_{1})|\Big)

\displaystyle\leq 2\Big(|(x_{1},y_{1})-(x_{2},y_{2})|+\text{Length}\Big(\gamma_{1}(x_{1},y_{1},x_{2},y_{2})\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle\leq 4\Big(|(x_{1},y_{1})-(x_{2},y_{2})|\Big).

So we conclude that

|(x_{1},y_{1})-(x_{2},y_{2})|\leq\text{Length}\Big(\gamma(x_{1},y_{1},x_{2},y_{2})\Big)\leq 5|(x_{1},y_{1})-(x_{2},y_{2})|,

with the lower bound being immediate and the upper bound following from the preceding calculations.
∎

Finally, we state a result about the transformation of lengths of curves under composition by a differentiable map.

Proposition 8.2
.

Let
f:U\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
be a
C^{1}
map, where
U\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
is an open set. If
\gamma
is a piecewise smooth curve in
U
, then

\text{Length}\Big(f(\gamma)\Big)\leq\|\nabla f\|_{L^{\infty}(U)}\cdot\text{Length}(\gamma).

Proof.

Consider the case where
\gamma
is smooth (the piecewise smooth case follows by considering each smooth piece separately), and let
\boldsymbol{r}(s)
for
a\leq s\leq b
be a parametrization of
\gamma
. Then,
f(\boldsymbol{r}(s))
is a parametrization of
f(\gamma)
. We compute

\text{Length}\Big(f(\gamma)\Big)=\int_{a}^{b}|\nabla f(\boldsymbol{r}(s))\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}(s)|ds\leq\|\nabla f\|_{L^{\infty}(U)}\int_{a}^{b}|\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}(s)|ds\leq\|\nabla f\|_{L^{\infty}(U)}\text{Length}(\gamma).

∎
